-- gaps_and_opportunities --
  * Generation gap: almost every 2026 mitigation/detection paper still evaluates on LLaVA-1.5-7B or InstructBLIP; there is no systematic object-hallucination study of the 2026 natively-multimodal small models (Qwen3.5-2B/4B, Gemma 4 E2B/E4B, LFM2.5-VL-3B, MiniCPM-V 4.6). A 'do 2024-25 training-free fixes (VCD/OPERA/AIR/ResDec) still help on 2026 <=4B models?' study is open and cheap.
  * Protocol gap: vendor HallusionBench numbers disagree with independent ones (Qwen3.5-4B 65.0 vendor vs 51.7 in Liquid's table; Qwen3.5-2B 58.0 thinking vs 51.3 non-thinking), and InternVL3.5-2B beats 4B on HallusionBench. A reproducible small-VLM hallucination table (VLMEvalKit/lmms-eval, thinking on/off, fixed prompts) would be cited.
  * Thinking-mode factor: RH-Bench and 'When Thinking Hurts' cover 7B+ reasoning models; the effect of the default thinking mode on object hallucination in <=4B models is unmeasured.
  * Quantization factor: GHOST-Q covered only 8B models at FP16/INT8/NF4; nobody has paired FP16 vs Q4-GGUF/NF4 predictions item-by-item on 2B-4B models - exactly what a GTX 1060 forces the student to do anyway.
  * Abstention-aware scoring: HumbleBench (MCQ 'None of the above'), DeflectionBench and 'To See or To Please' (zero robust refusal) show VLMs cannot abstain; no one has built a POPE-style existence test with an explicit 'cannot tell' option scored under a Kalai-style penalty rubric, nor compared small models on it.
  * Synthetic vs real: FREAK, CIPHER/OHC-25K, HII-DPO and POPEv2 all use diffusion-edited or masked images, but no controlled comparison exists of the same object probes on synthetic edits vs RePOPE-corrected real COCO images for 2026 small models (ODE 2025 did SD1.5 vs COCO on older models).
  * Trade-off reporting: 'Playing it Safe' (Sept 2026) shows hallucination gains often buy reduced informativeness; applying its faithfulness-informativeness-capability protocol to small models is unexplored.
  * Internal-state detection at small scale: HALP/InsLen/VisER probes are reported on 7B+ models; porting linear probes to 2B/4B hidden states is a direct LLM-Check bridge with no published baseline.
  * Emerging trends to name in the report: shift from contrastive decoding to representation editing/steering and mechanistic circuits (AIR, AFTER, CNS, ResOT, Dual-Pathway); pre-generation detection (HALP, InsLen, VisER, TruthLens, UniProbe); diffusion-edited counterfactual data; abstention/humility benchmarks; critiques of evaluation (RePOPE, Playing it Safe, GHOST-Q, Rethinking CD); span-level shared tasks (SHROOM-Visions).
  * Saturated areas to avoid: yet another training-free decoding tweak validated on LLaVA-1.5-7B with POPE/CHAIR (~40 such papers in 12 months); 'benchmark-of-everything' releases that need GPT-5/Gemini-scale evaluation.

-- recommended_for_this_project --
  * Model set (all fit a 6 GB card at 4-bit, all open licenses): Qwen/Qwen3.5-2B and Qwen3.5-4B (thinking on/off), Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct(-GGUF), OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-2B, google/gemma-4-E2B-it, LiquidAI/LFM2.5-VL-3B(-GGUF), openbmb/MiniCPM-V-4.6. Treat Gemma 4 E4B (8B with embeddings) as optional and 4-bit only. Avoid Moondream 3.1 (9B total, custom license, custom runtime), Isaac-0.2 (non-commercial), Phi-4-multimodal (5.6B).
  * Benchmarks: POPE with RePOPE labels (github.com/YanNeu/RePOPE), AMBER for generative CHAIR, HallusionBench via VLMEvalKit for comparability with the OpenVLM leaderboard, a HumbleBench subset for 'None of the above' abstention, plus a small (500-1,000 image) synthetic counterfactual set built with an open diffusion editor following FREAK's generate-then-edit or CIPHER/HII-DPO's object-removal recipe, with exact ground truth by construction.
  * Unique angle that fits 6-8 weeks: an abstention-aware, synthetic-vs-real object-existence benchmark for <=4B models, scored under a Kalai-style penalty rubric (wrong < abstain < right), with thinking-mode and Q4-vs-FP16 as controlled factors (GHOST-Q-style paired analysis). This bridges LLM-Check (probes), Kalai/Nature 2026 (incentives), HalluLens (NonExistentRefusal) to the multimodal setting and nobody has done it at small scale.
  * Mitigation baselines to replicate (code available): AIR (github.com/Ice-wave/AIR, CVPR 2026 Findings), VCD and OPERA (canonical 2024), SAVE (github.com/wiarae/SAVE), MaskCD/SAVCD/TCD/HDD (repos listed in the Awesome-LVLM-Hallucination list), Dual-Pathway suppression (github.com/jiaxin26/DualPath-VLM). Skip training-time DPO methods (FINER-Tuning on 14B, HII-DPO) unless cloud GPUs are budgeted; Obliviate-style LM-head-only unlearning is the one trainable option to consider on a 2B model.
  * Detection baselines: InsLen (github.com/Fraserlairh/Instruction-Lens-Score) and simple HALP-style linear probes on hidden states (no code, but the method is a logistic probe) - these mirror LLM-Check and need only forward passes.
  * Must-cite list for a late-2026 reviewer: Kalai et al. Nature 2026 (plus arXiv 2509.04664), HalluLens 2025, LLM-Check NeurIPS 2024, Huang et al. survey, Xu et al. 2024; CHAIR (Rohrbach 2018), POPE (Li et al. 2023, arXiv 2305.10355), RePOPE 2025, AMBER, HallusionBench (arXiv 2310.14566), VCD, OPERA, Woodpecker, HA-DPO, RLAIF-V; 2025-26: Survey of Multimodal Hallucination Evaluation and Detection (v2 Jan 2026), FREAK (ICLR 2026), CIPHER (CVPR 2026), FINER (CVPR 2026), AIR and ResDec (CVPR 2026), AFTER (ICLR 2026), HALP (EACL 2026), InsLen (ICML 2026), VisER/SAFE (EMNLP 2026), HumbleBench, VLM-DeflectionBench (ACL 2026), More Thinking Less Seeing (NeurIPS 2025), Playing it Safe and GHOST-Q (Sept 2026), SHROOM-Visions 2026.
  * Report the faithfulness-informativeness-capability trade-off (coverage/recall of true objects, caption length, MMStar or MMBench subset) alongside hallucination rates, following Fazli et al. 2026; reviewers now penalize CHAIR-only claims.
  * Compute: run POPE/HumbleBench yes-no and MCQ items with 4-bit models on the GTX 1060 (short outputs); reserve generative AMBER/CHAIR runs to a few hundred images per model; if 7B+ baselines are required, rent a single 24 GB cloud GPU for a few hours rather than train anything.

-- cautions --
  * WebSearch budget for this turn was exhausted after ~70 searches; Semantic Scholar API returned HTTP 429 twice, Hugging Face papers search returned 401, and the CVPR open-access page returned 403. Venue claims therefore rely on arXiv 'Comments' fields, conference virtual-site pages, and ACL Anthology pages; Papers with Code was not used (its listings were not consulted this turn).
  * Items not opened at a primary source (treat as unverified): KnowHal benchmark (arXiv 2608.03782), 'Rethinking the Effectiveness of Contrastive Decoding' (arXiv 2607.25196), TruthLens/UniProbe/PAS detectors (feed titles only), the Awesome list's PIH/CFHR/CSR-Bench/HalDec-Bench dates, FREAK's GitHub repo link, LFM2-VL-3B POPE 89.01, Qwen3-VL-4B-Thinking HallusionBench 64.1 (community gist, Oct 2025), unsloth GGUF ids, and the LFM license revenue threshold (from search snippets).
  * Date conflicts: the LFM2.5-VL-3B card summary returned 'Aug 12, 2025' but Liquid's blog is dated 12 Aug 2026 and compares against Gemma 4/Qwen3.5 (2026 models), so 2026 is correct. The Gemma 4 E2B card cites a July 2026 tech report (arXiv 2607.02770) while the HF launch blog says 2 Apr 2026. MiniCPM-V-4.6's card shows a '2026.05.17' API notice; GitHub news gives 2026-05-11 as the open-source date.
  * Vendor hallucination numbers are not comparable across cards: Qwen3.5-4B HallusionBench 65.0 (vendor, thinking) vs 51.7 (Liquid's protocol); MiniCPM-V 4.6's 63.2 at ~1B is vendor-reported; InternVL3.5 numbers come from the paper's own Table 6. Re-measure everything under one VLMEvalKit/lmms-eval protocol before drawing conclusions.
  * OpenVLM leaderboard values were read from a third-party mirror (benchmarklist.com, data dated 30 Jun 2026), not the live Gradio space; llm-stats POPE entries are explicitly self-reported (0 verified).
  * 'More Thinking, Less Seeing?' is labelled NeurIPS 2025 only because a proceedings.neurips.cc URL appeared in search results; its arXiv page lists no venue. FREAK's ICLR 2026 status likewise comes from the GitHub README title and an ICLR proceedings URL, not the arXiv abstract.
  * Many 2026 methods are preprints without released code (ResDec, CAST, FADE, SSAV, ResOT, HALP, VisER, DO-Bench, HII-DPO, Visual-Idk); plan experiments around the repos that exist (AIR, InsLen, SAFE, DualPath, RePOPE, HumbleBench, UniHall), none of which were opened to confirm they run.
  * The arXiv API feed used for 'newest work' is authoritative only for titles containing 'object hallucination' or 'hallucination' + VLM terms; papers using other wording (e.g., 'faithfulness', 'grounding') may be missed. Newest object-hallucination entry found: arXiv 2610.11826 (8 Oct 2026).
  * Moondream 3.1, Molmo2-4B (5B listed) and Gemma 4 E4B (8B with embeddings) exceed a strict 4B total-parameter limit; LLaVA-OneVision-1.5-4B-Instruct is listed as 5B on its card. No GTX 1060 memory measurements were taken; the 'fits at 4-bit' guidance is qualitative.

-- confidence_notes -- High confidence on: existence, dates, authors and venues of the 2026 papers opened on arXiv/ACL/ICLR/CVPR pages; exact HF model ids, licenses and release dates read from model cards and vendor READMEs; the Nature 2026 metadata (Crossref). Medium confidence on: venue labels taken only from arXiv comment fields (e.g., 'Accepted by ICML-2026'), NeurIPS 2025 for RH-Bench, and ICLR 2026 for FREAK. Low confidence on: third-party leaderboard numbers (benchmarklist mirror, llm-stats), community gist scores for Qwen3-VL, and any claim marked 'from search' in the items. Coverage of Oct 2025 - Oct 2026 arXiv activity is strong for titles containing 'object hallucination' (40 entries reviewed) and good for broader VLM-hallucination titles (Jun-Oct 2026 reviewed in full).


######## ANGLE: compute ########

-- local_gpu_findings --
  * GTX 1060 6GB = Pascal GP106, compute capability 6.1, 1280 CUDA cores, 6 GB GDDR5 (192 GB/s), ~4.4 TFLOPS FP32; native FP16 is 1:64 of FP32 (~68 GFLOPS), no tensor cores, no bf16. In practice PyTorch/cuBLAS fp16 on Pascal runs at roughly fp32 speed, so fp16 buys memory but not speed (community experience, estimate). Sources: https://www.techpowerup.com/gpu-specs/gigabyte-gtx-1060-rev-1-0.b3705 ; https://www.tomshardware.com/reviews/nvidia-geforce-gtx-1060-pascal,4679.html
  * Software cliff (verified Oct 2026): NVIDIA dropped Maxwell/Pascal/Volta from CUDA 13; PyTorch removed Pascal from its CUDA 12.8 wheels, and PyTorch 2.14 is the LAST release that ships CUDA 12.6 wheels (the only prebuilt ones with sm_60/sm_61 kernels). From PyTorch 2.15 a GTX 1060 user must pin `torch==2.14` from the cu126 index or build from source with TORCH_CUDA_ARCH_LIST=6.1. Sources: https://dev-discuss.pytorch.org/t/notice-cuda-12-6-wheels-will-no-longer-be-published-from-pytorch-2-15-drops-maxwell-pascal-volta/3432 ; https://dev-discuss.pytorch.org/t/cuda-toolkit-version-and-architecture-support-update-maxwell-and-pascal-architecture-support-removed-in-cuda-12-8-and-12-9-builds/3128 ; https://www.tomshardware.com/pc-components/gpus/nvidia-to-drop-cuda-support-for-maxwell-pascal-and-volta-gpus-with-the-next-major-toolkit-release
  * bitsandbytes (official docs, 2026): NF4/FP4 4-bit quantization and 8-bit optimizers need CC 6.0+ (Pascal OK), but LLM.int8() / load_in_8bit needs CC 7.5+ (Turing) -> on the 1060 use load_in_4bit (NF4), never 8-bit. PyPI wheels built for CUDA 11.8-12.6 include sm60 on Linux and sm50/sm60 on Windows; CUDA 12.8 wheels start at sm70 and CUDA 13.x wheels at sm75, so bitsandbytes must be paired with a cu126 PyTorch. Source: https://huggingface.co/docs/bitsandbytes/main/en/installation
  * vLLM refuses GPUs below CC 7.0 (a Pascal-enablement PR #4409 stalled because Triton tl.dot kernels do not run on sm_61); SGLang, FlashAttention-2 and Triton-based research code are likewise unusable on the 1060. Realistic stacks are HF transformers (eager/SDPA attention) and llama.cpp/Ollama (GGUF). Sources: https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/1284 ; https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/4409
  * llama.cpp multimodal (libmtmd via llama-mtmd-cli / llama-server) officially lists Gemma 3 (4B/12B/27B), Gemma 4 (E2B/E4B/26B-A4B/31B), SmolVLM/SmolVLM2 (256M/500M/2.2B), Qwen2-VL (2B/7B), Qwen2.5-VL (3B/7B/32B/72B), InternVL2.5/3 (1B/2B/4B/8B/14B), Moondream2, Pixtral 12B, Mistral Small 3.1, Llama 4 Scout and Qwen2.5-Omni 3B; the vision projector (mmproj) is offloaded to GPU by default (`--no-mmproj-offload` frees VRAM at a speed cost). Qwen3-VL GGUFs also exist (HF card says 'use in llama.cpp, Ollama, LM Studio'; Ollama ships qwen3-vl:2b at 1.9 GB and :4b at 3.3 GB). Sources: https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/docs/multimodal.md ; https://ollama.com/library/qwen3-vl ; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct
  * Measured llama.cpp CUDA speed on a GTX 1060 6GB (Llama-2-7B Q4_0, community scoreboard): prompt processing pp512 = 417-446 tok/s, generation tg128 = 28 tok/s, versus RTX 4090 = 11,993-14,771 / 186-189 tok/s and RTX 3090 = 5,175-5,560 / 158-162 tok/s. So the 1060 is ~30x slower than a 4090 on prefill (which is what image tokens cost) and ~6.7x slower on decode. Source: https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/discussions/15013
  * Ollama supports NVIDIA CC 5.0+ and explicitly lists CC 6.1 cards including the GTX 1060 (driver >= 570 required for CC 5.0-6.2). Vision models that fit in 6 GB as Q4 pulls: qwen3-vl:2b (1.9 GB), qwen3-vl:4b (3.3 GB), gemma3:4b (3.4 GB; the gemma3 270m/1b tags are text-only). qwen3-vl:8b (6.1 GB) leaves no room for KV cache + image tokens. Sources: https://docs.ollama.com/gpu ; https://ollama.com/library/qwen3-vl ; https://ollama.com/library/gemma3
  * Official VRAM figures for the smallest VLM family: SmolVLM-256M needs 0.8 GB for single-image inference and SmolVLM2-2.2B needs 4.9 GB (video: 1.38 GB / 5.2 GB). The 2.2B model is about the largest you can run unquantized in fp16 on 6 GB. Sources: https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM-256M-Instruct ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05299
  * VRAM arithmetic for 448-1024 px inputs (estimate, no official table): fp16 weights = 2 bytes/param (1B ~2 GB, 2B ~4-4.5 GB; 3B+ does not fit in fp16 -> NF4 ~0.6 bytes/param plus an fp16 ViT of 0.4-1.3 GB). Qwen2/2.5/3-VL image tokens ~ (H/28)*(W/28)/4 -> ~256 tokens at 448 px, ~1,300 at 1024 px; KV cache + activations for a 2B model at 1,500 tokens ~0.3-0.5 GB. Practical rule: <=2B fp16 or <=4B NF4/Q4 at 448-768 px; 1024 px only for <=2B models and only with max_pixels capped (e.g. 640x640) so prefill stays at 1-2 s.
  * Hidden states and attention maps (LLM-Check-style) ARE extractable on the 1060: output_hidden_states works with any attention backend, but output_attentions is only returned by the 'eager' implementation (transformers warns and falls back to eager; for Qwen2.5-VL eager vs SDPA logits differ slightly). Memory: one layer's attention map = heads x L x L x 2 bytes ~ 16 x 1,200^2 x 2 ~ 46 MB, ~1.3 GB if all 28 layers are kept, so capture with forward hooks and move each layer to CPU immediately (estimate). Sources: https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/en/attention_interface ; https://github.com/huggingface/transformers/issues/41924 ; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct/discussions/53
  * Stable Diffusion 1.5 on a GTX 1060 6GB: 1.29-1.47 it/s at 512x512 (Euler a, 30 steps, --xformers) = roughly 20-25 s per image; SD 1.5 inpainting checkpoints (runwayml/stable-diffusion-inpainting, dreamshaper-8-inpainting) fit the same budget at 512 px. The Salad SD 1.5 benchmark measured a 598 s cold start on the 1060 and warns that GTX cards can emit all-black images in fp16 (use fp32 VAE / --no-half-vae). Sources: https://github.com/AUTOMATIC1111/stable-diffusion-webui/discussions/10308 ; https://blog.salad.com/stable-diffusion-v1-5-benchmark/
  * SDXL on 6 GB is marginal: the Fooocus README lists GTX 10xx cards as needing 8 GB ('some people report 6GB success on GTX 10XX, but some other people report failure cases') while RTX cards need only 4 GB; ComfyUI --lowvram streams weights from system RAM and works, but expect minutes per 1024^2 image (estimate). FLUX.1-schnell (12B) requires a GGUF Q4 file (~6.8 GB, larger than VRAM) plus --lowvram offload; third-party guides report 60-90 s/image on 8 GB cards, so expect 2-5 min/image on the 1060 (estimate; fp8 kernels unavailable on Pascal). Sources: https://github.com/lllyasviel/Fooocus/blob/main/readme.md ; https://localaimaster.com/blog/run-flux-on-low-vram-gpu ; https://huggingface.co/Niansuh/FLUX.1-schnell-gguf/blob/main/README.md
  * LaMa inpainting (object removal for 'remove the object, ask if it is still there' probes) is trivially feasible: IOPaint runs it on CPU or GPU (`iopaint start --model=lama --device=cpu`) and also wraps SD/SDXL-inpainting, BrushNet, PowerPaint and Paint-by-Example; the LaMa Fourier-convolution network is tens of millions of parameters, far under 1 GB of VRAM (estimate). Sources: https://github.com/Sanster/IOPaint ; https://github.com/advimman/lama
  * Open-vocabulary detectors/segmenters fit in 6 GB if loaded one at a time: Grounding DINO tiny (IDEA-Research/grounding-dino-tiny, 0.2B params; third-party guide: ~1.2 GB for tiny, ~3.8 GB for base, base fp32 peaks ~5.3 GB at 640 px) -> use tiny or fp16; OWLv2 base (google/owlv2-base-patch16-ensemble, 0.2B; ~1.5 GB incl. activations per third-party); SAM ViT-H (facebook/sam-vit-huge, 0.6B, 1024^2 input) is heavy -> prefer facebook/sam-vit-base or SAM 2 hiera-small (~4 GB) / tiny (~2.5 GB) per third-party. Unload the VLM before running the detector pipeline. Sources: https://huggingface.co/IDEA-Research/grounding-dino-tiny ; https://huggingface.co/google/owlv2-base-patch16-ensemble ; https://huggingface.co/facebook/sam-vit-huge ; https://theneuralbase.com/grounding-dino/learn/beginner/gpu-requirements/ ; https://theneuralbase.com/sam/learn/beginner/gpu-requirements/
  * Decoding-time mitigation overheads measured on LLaVA-1.5-7B with eager attention: greedy 61.3 ms/step, VCD 123.2 ms (2.01x), OPERA 435.7 ms (7.12x); contrastive methods always need two forward passes per token and OPERA adds beam search + rollback. On the 1060 this multiplies already slow decoding, so run VCD-class (2x) methods locally and treat OPERA as cloud-only. Sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2605.24024 (Table 6) ; VCD https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.16922 ; OPERA https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.17911
  * Throughput ESTIMATE for 2-4B VLMs on the 1060 via llama.cpp (derived from the 7B scoreboard numbers, not directly measured): image encoding 1-3 s + prefill ~1 s per 1,000 tokens + decode at ~40-60 tok/s -> POPE-style yes/no ~3-5 s/image (12-20 images/min); 100-token captions ~5-8 s/image (8-12 images/min). HF transformers + bitsandbytes NF4 at batch 1 is typically 2-4x slower on this GPU. Benchmark 50 images before planning; a Kaggle T4 (16 GB, tensor cores) should be roughly 3-6x faster (estimate).
  * Datacenter reference throughput for cost estimates: vLLM on one A100-40GB with image inputs sustains 13.2 req/s for Qwen2.5-VL-7B, 15.6 for InternVL3.5-4B, 26.3 for InternVL3.5-2B and 17.6 for MiniCPM-V-4 (ShareGPT4V prompts, ~100-180 output tokens); a third-party vLLM run reports ~7.5 req/s for Qwen2.5-VL-7B on an RTX 4090. Sources: https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/24728 ; https://www.databasemart.com/blog/vllm-gpu-benchmark-rtx4090

-- free_tiers_and_credits --
  * Kaggle: 30 free GPU-hours/week on a P100 16GB or 2x T4 16GB, 12-hour sessions, 20 GB disk, no credit card. This is the single most valuable free resource for the project (~360 GPU-hours over 12 weeks); a T4 has 16 GB plus tensor cores/bf16, i.e. it beats the GTX 1060 on everything except persistence. https://docs.ultralytics.com/integrations/kaggle/ ; https://www.kaggle.com/docs/efficient-gpu-usage
  * Google Colab: free tier gives heavily restricted T4 access with 12-hour max runtimes; Colab Pro is $9.99/month for 100 compute units (~80 T4-hours or ~18 A100-40GB-hours at third-party measured burn rates), Pro+ $49.99 for 600 CU. Since Sept 22, 2026 paid Google AI plans deposit Colab compute units monthly (AI Ultra adds background execution + premium GPUs); exact CU counts are not in the official post. https://research.google.com/colaboratory/faq.html ; https://developers.googleblog.com/colab-is-now-part-of-your-google-ai-plan/
  * Lightning AI free Studios: ~15 credits/month (~22 T4-hours), no card required, one always-on CPU studio; figures are third-party because the official pricing page did not render. https://aicreditmart.com/ai-credits-providers/lightning-ai-free-plan-22-gpu-hours-month-guide-2026/ ; https://lightning.ai/pricing
  * GitHub Student Developer Pack (official page, Oct 2026): Microsoft Azure $100 credit (18+), Heroku $13/month for 24 months, GitHub Copilot Student, Codespaces Pro, Camber (40 CPU-h + 5 GPU-h/month), MongoDB Atlas $50, Deepnote Team. DigitalOcean's $200 credit left the pack (expired Aug 1, 2026 per GitHub Community discussion and third-party trackers). https://education.github.com/pack ; https://github.com/orgs/community/discussions/201240
  * Azure for Students: $100 credit valid 12 months, renewable yearly while a student, no card, plus 750 h of B-series CPU VMs. Caveat: N-series (GPU) vCPU quota is 0 on student subscriptions and increase requests are frequently refused; Microsoft Q&A answers say upgrade to pay-as-you-go to get GPUs. Treat it as credit for CPU preprocessing, storage and hosting a judge/annotation API, not for GPUs. https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/free/students/ ; https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/answers/questions/1471964/if-im-in-the-students-subscription-and-i-request-a
  * AWS: AWS Educate gives free training, labs and badges but no credits. New AWS accounts on the Free plan get $100 in credits at sign-up plus up to $100 more for activities, valid 6 months, but GPU instances such as g4dn are not free-tier eligible and need the paid plan plus a G-family vCPU quota increase. https://aws.amazon.com/education/awseducate/ ; https://aws.amazon.com/free/ ; https://cloudprice.net/aws/ec2/instances/g4dn.xlarge
  * Google Cloud: $300 welcome credit for 90 days, but the docs state 'While your billing account is a non-billable Free trial account, you can't ... Add GPUs to your VM instances' and 'GPUs and TPUs are not included in the Free Tier' - you must upgrade to a paid billing account (unused credit carries over) and then request GPU quota. Student-specific Google Cloud credit programs could not be verified (page truncated). https://docs.cloud.google.com/free/docs/free-cloud-features ; https://cloud.google.com/edu/students
  * Gemini API free tier: Gemini 3.8 Flash, 3.5 Flash-Lite, 2.5 Flash and 2.5 Flash-Lite all list 'free of charge' input/output tokens on the free tier (rate-limited) - enough for pilot LLM-as-judge runs of a few hundred images/day; check the data-use terms before sending anything sensitive. https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/pricing
  * Hugging Face: a PRO subscription (~$9/month) includes ZeroGPU Spaces quota and higher Inference limits - plausible for demos, but NOT verified this session; confirm on huggingface.co/pricing before relying on it.

-- worked_examples --
  * (a) Evaluate 5 small VLMs (<=4B) on 3 benchmarks x 3,000 images = 45,000 inferences. Assumptions: images <=768 px (~600-900 image tokens), ~900 input tokens avg, ~60 output tokens avg (POPE/AMBER yes-no ~5 tokens, CHAIR captions ~120 tokens), all models fit a 24 GB card in bf16. RTX 4090 ($0.34-0.89/hr): with vLLM batching (A100-40GB measured 13-26 req/s for 2B-7B VLMs; a 4090 conservatively 5-10 req/s) pure compute is 1.3-2.5 h; with plain HF transformers at batch 4-8 expect ~1-3 img/s -> 4-12 h. Add ~50% for downloads (COCO val2014 ~6 GB, models 10-20 GB) and debugging -> 2-18 h -> $1-16; budget $5-25 incl. a persistent network volume. A100 80GB ($1.07 Vast / $1.79 RunPod / $2.79 Lambda): same hours -> $2-50; budget $10-40. The A100 buys nothing over a 4090 for <=4B models; pick the 4090 (or the $0.27/hr RTX A5000 on RunPod).
  * (b) Generate or edit 2,000 synthetic images. Cloud RTX 4090: SD 1.5 at 512 px ~1.6-3 s/img (Salad: 1.63 s best case) -> ~1-1.5 h; SDXL 1024 px at 30 steps ~5-8 s/img (estimate) -> 3-4.5 h; FLUX.1-schnell 4-step ~2-5 s/img (estimate) -> 1-3 h; an object-removal/insertion pipeline (Grounding DINO -> SAM mask -> LaMa or SD-inpaint -> detector re-check) ~3-6 s/img -> 2-4 h. With 2-3 candidates per image for quality filtering, total 4090 cost is about $5-20; A100 $10-40. API alternative: Gemini 3.1 Flash Image is ~$0.067 per 1K image -> ~$134 for 2,000 images (official pricing) - use only for a few hundred hard edits. Local GTX 1060: SD 1.5 at 512 px ~20-25 s/img -> 11-14 h for 2,000 (two overnight runs, feasible); LaMa removal ~1-2 s/img on GPU, also runs on CPU -> <2 h; SDXL via --lowvram ~2-4 min/img -> 70-130 h and FLUX schnell GGUF ~2-5 min/img -> 70-170 h (both impractical locally).
  * (c) Three training-free mitigation methods on 2 models x 2,000 images, captioning with max 128 new tokens. Measured overheads on LLaVA-1.5-7B: VCD 2.01x, OPERA 7.12x (arXiv 2605.24024 Table 6); assume a third method at ~2x (ICD/DoLa-style) and a baseline run. Official OPERA/VCD repos are batch-1 HF code, so throughput does not improve on bigger GPUs. 4090 baseline ~3 s/img for a 7B fp16 model or ~1.5-2 s for a 3B model: 4,000 images x (1 + 2.01 + 7.12 + 2) x 1.5-3 s = 20-40 h -> $7-36 at $0.34-0.89/hr (A100: 18-36 h -> $20-100). If OPERA is swapped for a ~1.2x attention-steering method the total drops to ~6x baseline -> 10-20 h -> $3-18. Recommended: evaluate mitigations on 500 images per model (the common CHAIR protocol) -> 5-10 h -> $2-9 on a 4090. Local GTX 1060: bnb-NF4 LLaVA-1.5-7B decodes at maybe 3-6 tok/s (estimate), so the full 4,000-image x 12x run would take weeks; a 3B model via HF fp16/NF4 at ~8-10 s/caption gives 4,000 x 12 x 9 s = 120 h -> only subsets (<=300 images) are realistic locally.
  * (d) 10,000 LLM-as-judge calls at ~1,000 input tokens (image included) + 150 output tokens. Costs = 10x the per-1,000 column: GPT-5-nano $1.10, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite $1.60, Claude Haiku 5.5 $1.75, GPT-4o-mini $2.40, GPT-5.4-nano $3.90, GPT-5-mini $5.50, GPT-4.1-mini $6.40, Gemini 2.5 Flash / 3.5 Flash-Lite $6.75, Gemini 3.8 Flash $13.10 (intro; $26 from 2027), GPT-5.4-mini $14.30, Claude Haiku 4.5 $17.50, GPT-5 $27.50, Gemini 2.5 Pro $27.50, GPT-4.1 $32, Claude Sonnet 5.5 $35, Gemini 3.1 Pro $38, GPT-4o $40, GPT-5.4 $47.50, Claude Sonnet 4.6 $52.50, Claude Opus 5.5 $70, GPT-5.5 $95. Batch APIs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google) halve all of these. Image-token reality check: a 1024x1024 image costs GPT-5 ~630 tokens (70 + 4 tiles x 140), GPT-4o/4.1 ~765, Gemini ~1,032 (4 x 258), Claude ~1,369 (37x37 patches of 28 px) - so 1,000 input tokens holds only for <=1 MP images; downscaling judge images to 512 px cuts input to ~200-350 tokens. Plan: pilot 300 calls on 2-3 judges against human labels, then run 10k on the cheapest judge with >=90% agreement -> realistic spend $5-50.
  * (e) The same evaluation as (a) entirely on the local GTX 1060 (45,000 inferences). Using the llama.cpp scoreboard (1060: pp512 ~420-450 tok/s and tg ~28 tok/s for 7B Q4; roughly 2x that for 2-4B models), per image ~1-3 s image encoding + ~1 s prefill (900 tokens) + 0.1-3 s decode (5-120 tokens at ~45 tok/s) = ~2.5-7 s -> 45,000 x 2.5-7 s = 31-88 h = 1.3-3.7 days of uninterrupted running; realistically 5-9 calendar days with crashes, re-runs and the machine being a shared second PC. With HF transformers + bitsandbytes NF4 (required for logits, hidden states and custom decoding) expect 2-4x slower: 3-15 days. Electricity is negligible (~120 W x 88 h = ~11 kWh). Conclusion: do the full 5x3x3,000 sweep on Kaggle (30 free T4-hours/week; a T4 is ~3-6x faster and has 16 GB) or a $5-25 RTX 4090 rental, and reserve the 1060 for development, <=500-image subsets, and the hidden-state/attention (LLM-Check-style) experiments where interactivity matters more than throughput. All 1060 timings here are derived estimates - measure 50 images first.

-- cautions --
  * All prices are as of Oct 12, 2026 and move monthly. Specific landmines: Gemini 3.8 Flash's $0.75/$3.75 is an introductory price that doubles on Jan 1, 2027; Claude Sonnet 5's $2/$10 intro price was made permanent; OpenAI lists both GPT-5.x and legacy GPT-4.1/4o at the old prices but may retire them.
  * RunPod's official page shows one price per GPU (RTX 4090 $0.89/hr, 'Updated Sept 27, 2026') without a clear Secure vs Community label, while an aggregator dated Oct 12, 2026 lists RunPod's 4090 at $0.34/hr on-demand. Vast.ai's official pricing page shows no numbers at all (live grid); its $0.38-0.43/hr 4090 and $1.07/hr A100 figures are from third-party trackers. Check the console price before launching.
  * Unverified from official pages (third-party only): Lightning AI free-tier figures (official pricing page did not render), Colab Pro/Pro+ prices and compute-unit burn rates (sign-up page requires login), the '200 CU/month with Google AI Pro' claim, DigitalOcean's exit from the GitHub Student Pack, Google Cloud student credits, Hugging Face ZeroGPU quota, the openbmb/MiniCPM-V-4 int4 repo, Qwen3.5-0.8B/2B ids, and LaMa's parameter count.
  * Pascal software cliff: PyTorch 2.14 is the last release with CUDA 12.6 wheels (the only ones containing sm_61); bitsandbytes must be the CUDA <=12.6 wheel; vLLM, SGLang, FlashAttention-2, Triton kernels and most 2025-26 research repos that assume bf16/FA2 will not run on the 1060 without patches. Brand-new models (Gemma 4, Qwen3.5) require transformers from source, which may pull a PyTorch > 2.14 - pin versions in a dedicated venv.
  * No bf16 on Pascal: models trained in bf16 (Gemma 3/4, Qwen3-VL, Qwen3.5, InternVL3.5) must be loaded as float16 and can overflow (NaN/garbage) in some layers; verify outputs on a handful of images and fall back to fp32 for the vision tower if needed. SD/SDXL on GTX cards can produce black images with an fp16 VAE.
  * All GTX 1060 throughput figures for VLMs are derived estimates (scaled from the llama.cpp 7B scoreboard); no published benchmark of a VLM on a 1060 was found. SDXL and FLUX timings on the 1060 are estimates from 8 GB-card reports. Measure 50 images before committing to a plan.
  * Decoding-time mitigation code (OPERA, VCD, HALC) is pinned to LLaVA-1.5/InstructBLIP/MiniGPT-4 and old transformers versions, runs at batch 1, and OPERA's beam search is 7x slower than greedy; porting these to a 2-4B modern VLM is itself a week of work and is best done on a cloud GPU, not the 1060. Prefer VCD-class (2x) or attention-steering (~1.2x) methods for anything run locally.
  * The web-search budget for this run was exhausted before the Tom's Hardware SD benchmark, GIGAGPU page and a dedicated SDXL-on-1060 report could be retrieved; those gaps are marked as estimates. OpenAI's openai.com/api/pricing and Colab's sign-up page returned 403/login walls; OpenAI prices come from developers.openai.com instead.
  * Student credit traps: Azure for Students has GPU quota 0 and requests are commonly refused; the AWS Free plan excludes GPU instances; the Google Cloud $300 trial forbids attaching GPUs until the account is upgraded. None of these give usable GPU time without a credit card and a quota request.
  * Spot/interruptible instances (Vast.ai, RunPod spot) can be killed mid-run: write results as JSONL per image, resume by image id, and keep datasets and model caches on a persistent network volume ($0.05-0.10/GB-month) to avoid re-downloading 20+ GB each session.
  * Gemini free-tier calls may be used by Google to improve products; use the paid tier (or OpenAI/Anthropic) for any images or outputs that cannot be shared. LLM-as-judge results should be validated against a human-labelled subset before being reported (Kalai et al. 2025 argue evaluation design itself rewards guessing - a judge that cannot abstain inherits the bias).
  * Qwen3-VL is not in llama.cpp's official multimodal example list even though community/official GGUFs exist and Ollama ships it; if the llama.cpp CUDA build on the 1060 fails for a given mmproj, fall back to Ollama (which still ships a CUDA 12 path that includes compute 6.1 per third-party reports) or to transformers.

-- local_gpu_feasible_models --
  * SmolVLM-256M-Instruct (and SmolVLM2-256M-Video-Instruct) | HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM-256M-Instruct | 256M | transformers fp16/fp32, or llama.cpp GGUF ggml-org/SmolVLM-256M-Instruct-GGUF | ~0.8 GB | est. 30-60 (short answers), 15-25 (captions) | Official: 0.8 GB single-image inference. Too weak to be a headline model but ideal for pipeline debugging and for cheap hidden-state/attention (LLM-Check-style) experiments; hallucinates heavily, which makes it a useful high-hallucination baseline. https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM-256M-Instruct
  * SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct | HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct | 2.2B | transformers fp16 (fits unquantized) or llama.cpp GGUF ggml-org/SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct-GGUF | ~4.9 GB | est. 8-15 (short), 4-8 (captions) | Official 4.9 GB single-image figure; set do_image_splitting=False to keep image tokens and VRAM down. Largest model runnable in fp16 on 6 GB; good for full-precision logit/hidden-state work. https://huggingface.co/HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct
  * Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct | Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct | 3.75B | transformers + bitsandbytes NF4 (fp16 ViT), or llama.cpp GGUF ggml-org/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct-GGUF (Q4_K_M + fp16 mmproj) | ~4 GB | est. 8-15 (short, 448 px), 4-6 (captions) | Estimate: ~2.5 GB NF4 LLM + ~1.3 GB fp16 ViT + KV; cap max_pixels (e.g. 448x448=200,704 or 640x640) because image tokens scale with pixels/784. Does not fit in fp16 (7.5 GB). Widely used in 2025-26 hallucination papers, so it is a natural anchor model. https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct
  * Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct | Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct | 2B | transformers fp16 (needs transformers >= 4.57), or Ollama qwen3-vl:2b (1.9 GB Q4) / community GGUFs | ~5 GB | est. 10-20 (short, Q4 via llama.cpp), 5-8 (captions) | fp16 weights ~4.4 GB (estimate) leave little headroom -> keep images <=640 px or use the Q4 GGUF (~2.5-3 GB). bf16-native: load as float16 and check for NaNs on Pascal. Released Oct 2025; 256K context. https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct ; https://ollama.com/library/qwen3-vl
  * Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct | Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct | 4B | transformers + bitsandbytes NF4, or Ollama qwen3-vl:4b (3.3 GB Q4) | ~4.5 GB | est. 6-12 (short), 3-5 (captions) | fp16 does not fit (8+ GB). Q4 pull is 3.3 GB plus ViT/KV (estimate 4-5 GB total). Strong small model (MMMU 67.4 per third-party summaries); slow prefill on the 1060 at 1024 px. https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct ; https://ollama.com/library/qwen3-vl
  * Qwen3.5-4B (natively multimodal; 0.8B/2B siblings) | Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B | 4B (siblings 0.8B, 2B, 9B) | transformers from source (latest required) with NF4, or Q4 GGUF once available | ~4.5 GB | est. 6-12 (short); untested on Pascal | Feb 2026 release: 'Causal Language Model with Vision Encoder', early-fusion, claims to outperform Qwen3-VL. Newest option for a 'latest models' angle, but requires bleeding-edge transformers that may demand PyTorch > 2.14 (conflict with Pascal). Sibling ids (Qwen/Qwen3.5-2B, -0.8B) inferred from the 4B card's benchmark list - verify. https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B
  * InternVL3.5-1B / InternVL3.5-2B | OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-1B | 1.1B (0.3B ViT + 0.8B LLM); 2B sibling | transformers fp16 (trust_remote_code); InternVL3-1B/2B GGUFs in llama.cpp (ggml-org/InternVL3-1B-Instruct-GGUF) | ~2.5 GB | est. 15-25 (short), 6-10 (captions) | Estimate for fp16 at max_num=1-4 tiles (448 px tiles, 256 tokens each). Fastest model in the vLLM A100 benchmark (26 req/s for the 2B). Apache-2.0, released Aug 2025. https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-1B
  * Gemma 3 4B it | google/gemma-3-4b-it | 4.3B | Ollama gemma3:4b (3.4 GB Q4) / llama.cpp ggml-org/gemma-3-4b-it-GGUF, or transformers NF4 | ~4.5 GB | est. 6-12 (short), 3-5 (captions) | Fixed 896 px SigLIP with 256 image tokens -> cheap prefill on the 1060. bf16-native; fp16 overflow risk on Pascal (test). Gemma license (not Apache). https://ollama.com/library/gemma3 ; https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/docs/multimodal.md
  * Gemma 4 E2B-it (April 2026) | google/gemma-4-E2B-it | 2.3B effective / 5.1B total (PLE embeddings) | llama.cpp ggml-org/gemma-4-E2B-it-GGUF (officially listed) or latest transformers | ~4 GB | est. 8-15 (short); untested on Pascal | Image + audio + text input, 128K context; the E4B sibling also has a GGUF. Estimate assumes Q4 with per-layer embeddings resident on CPU; unverified on Pascal. Newest small VLM you can cite. https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-E2B-it ; https://huggingface.co/blog/gemma4
  * Moondream2 (rev 2025-06-21) | vikhyatk/moondream2 | ~2B | transformers fp16 (trust_remote_code) or llama.cpp ggml-org/moondream2-20250414-GGUF | ~4 GB | est. 10-20 (short) | Estimate. Has built-in detect() / point() APIs, handy for checking whether a model can ground the objects it names. Moondream 3 preview exists (9B MoE, 2B active) but is too large for 6 GB in fp16. https://huggingface.co/vikhyatk/moondream2
  * LFM2-VL-1.6B (and LFM2-VL-450M) | LiquidAI/LFM2-VL-1.6B | 1.6B (1.2B LM + 0.4B SigLIP2 NaFlex) | transformers fp16 | ~3.5 GB | est. 12-25 (short), 6-10 (captions) | Estimate. Native 512x512 tiles with a user-tunable image-token budget, advertised 2x faster GPU inference than peers - a good fit for a slow GPU. Released Nov 2025. https://huggingface.co/LiquidAI/LFM2-VL-1.6B
  * LLaVA-1.5-7B (the model OPERA/VCD/HALC official code targets) | llava-hf/llava-1.5-7b-hf | 7B | transformers + bitsandbytes NF4 (load_in_4bit), or llama.cpp GGUF (no custom decoding) | ~5.5 GB | est. 3-6 (short), 1.5-3 (captions) | Borderline: ~3.5-4 GB NF4 weights + 576 image tokens + KV (estimate). Feasible for POPE/CHAIR at batch 1 with max_new_tokens <= 128 and VCD (2 passes); OPERA's 5-beam search will likely OOM on 6 GB. bitsandbytes 4-bit matmuls are slow on Pascal. https://huggingface.co/llava-hf/llava-1.5-7b-hf
  * MiniCPM-V-4 | openbmb/MiniCPM-V-4 | ~4B | transformers + bitsandbytes NF4 (an official int4 repo may exist - not re-verified this session) | ~4.5 GB | est. 6-12 (short) | Estimate. Appears in the vLLM A100 benchmark (17.6 req/s). Ollama lists minicpm-v4.5 (8B, too big) and a 1B minicpm-v4.6 tag (not verified). https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/issues/24728

-- confidence_notes -- High confidence (official pages fetched Oct 12, 2026): bitsandbytes hardware table; llama.cpp docs/multimodal.md model list; Ollama GPU docs and library sizes; RunPod, Lambda and DigitalOcean price tables; OpenAI (developers.openai.com), Anthropic (platform.claude.com) and Gemini (ai.google.dev) per-token prices and image-token rules; GitHub Student Pack and Azure for Students terms; Google Cloud free-trial GPU restriction; PyTorch dev-discuss CUDA 12.6 deprecation notice; llama.cpp CUDA scoreboard figures for the GTX 1060/3090/4090; SmolVLM official VRAM numbers; HF model cards for Qwen3-VL-2B/4B, Qwen3.5-4B, Gemma 4 E2B, InternVL3.5-1B, LFM2-VL-1.6B, Moondream2, LLaVA-1.5-7B, grounding-dino-tiny, owlv2-base, sam-vit-huge; the vLLM A100 multimodal benchmark; the OPERA/VCD 7.12x/2.01x overhead table (arXiv 2605.24024); the GTX 1060 SD 1.5 it/s report (A1111 discussion). Medium confidence (reputable third-party trackers or blogs): Vast.ai and cross-provider GPU prices, Colab prices and compute-unit burn rates, Kaggle quota (via Ultralytics docs), Lightning AI free tier, Grounding DINO/SAM VRAM figures, FLUX-on-8GB timings, DigitalOcean leaving the student pack, Azure student GPU-quota refusals. Low confidence / explicit estimates: every VLM images-per-minute figure on the 1060, VRAM estimates derived from parameter counts, SDXL/FLUX timings on the 1060, cloud hours in worked examples (a)-(c), and the suggestion that a Kaggle T4 is 3-6x faster than the 1060. The judge-cost column is arithmetic on official prices (1,000 input + 150 output tokens per call) and is exact for those assumptions. The web-search quota ran out near the end; a follow-up message can continue the search if the student wants the remaining gaps (Tom's Hardware SD benchmark, SDXL-on-1060 reports, official Lightning/Colab pages) closed.


######## ANGLE: venues ########
angle: Publishable, undergrad-feasible contribution in LVLM object hallucination: the "minimum credible unit" derived from 2024-2026 short/workshop-scale papers, where to submit in 2026-2027, and the reviewer criticisms to pre-empt.

[1] Does Playing it Safe Count as Faithfulness? Reassessing LVLM Hallucination Mitigation Methods (2026; arXiv preprint (cs.CV), Sept 2026) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01888
    what: Analysis-only paper re-evaluating six training-free mitigation methods (VCD, M3ID, AGLA, CAAC, CEI, AFTER) on three LVLMs (LLaVA-1.5-7B, LLaVA-NeXT-7B, InstructBLIP-7B) over CHAIR (500 COCO images), AMBER (1,004 images) and MMStar (1,500 MCQs). Finds lower CHAIR co-occurs with lower object recall/coverage, i.e. evaluations 'may overestimate progress by rewarding conservative generation'. Compute: one A100-80GB; no new human annotation (reuses AMBER's human object lists).
    why: This is the exact shape of a feasible undergraduate paper: no training, 3 models x 6 methods x 3 benchmarks. It is also the multimodal mirror of Kalai et al.'s argument (evaluations reward confident guessing): here evaluations reward saying less, so it bridges the advisor's text-only list directly.
    code/data: none found on the abs page

[2] What Makes 'Good' Distractors for Object Hallucination Evaluation (HOPE) (2025; arXiv (Aug 2025), no venue stated) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06530
    what: Benchmark/analysis paper showing POPE's random/popular/adversarial negatives are too easy for 2025 models; builds image-specific distractors (CLIP-likely objects, true object + false description) and reports a 9-23% precision drop on 6 LVLMs from 3 families (Qwen2-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, LLaVA-NeXT, LLaVA-OneVision, InternVL2.5, InternVL3). Data: 2,000 Objects365 images plus COCO/VG/OpenImages text; manual verification of generated descriptions; no GPU specs given.
    why: Establishes that a benchmark-construction paper with ~2k images, 6 open models and zero training is a credible unit, and is the key citation for the 'distractor quality / yes-no bias' criticism of POPE.
    code/data: https://github.com/xiemk/HOPE (no license shown)

[3] Can They Still See Removed Objects? (ROHE benchmark + oDPO) (2025; NAACL 2025 (Long)) verified=True
    url: https://aclanthology.org/2025.naacl-long.349/
    what: Uses LaMa inpainting to remove objects from real COCO images, forming positive/negative VQA pairs; 5,504 human-checked evaluation units; evaluates 13 LVLM variants (LLaVA-1.5/1.6 7B-34B, InstructBLIP, Qwen-VL-Chat, SPHINX, Monkey-Chat, LLaVA-MOF, VW-LMM); then trains oDPO with LoRA on one A100-80GB (LLaVA-1.5-7B acc+ 39.21 -> 61.65). Also reports Object HalBench, MME-Hall, AMBER, MMHalBench.
    why: Closest template for 'synthetic AND real visual data': edited-real images as controlled negatives. The benchmark half is doable on your budget (LaMa is light; the human check can be your own), the DPO half is not.
    code/data: none listed on the Anthology page (check the PDF)

[4] Summary-Guided Decoding (SumGD) (2025; Findings of NAACL 2025 (arXiv 2410.13321)) verified=True
    url: https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-naacl.235/
    what: Training-free decoding that summarises prior text to curb language priors. Tested on LLaVA-1.5 7B/13B and InstructBLIP 7B/13B (+LLaVA-1.6-7B); CHAIR on 200 COCO val images at fixed lengths 64/128/256/512 plus GPT-4-judged SHR on 200 Visual Genome images; baselines greedy/nucleus/beam/VCD/ICD/M3ID/OPERA; all on one RTX A6000 (48GB).
    why: Documents the standard footprint of a Findings-level mitigation paper: two model families, 200-image CHAIR, one 48GB GPU, and explicit precision-recall (Pareto) reporting that reviewers now expect.
    code/data: not listed on the Anthology page; arXiv https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.13321

[5] Minimal training-free decoding papers: LayerCD (arXiv) and IBD (CVPR 2025 Workshop) (2025; LayerCD: arXiv Sept 2025; IBD: CVPR 2025 Workshops (TMM-OpenWorld), arXiv 2402.18476) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25177
    what: LayerCD contrasts shallow vs deep vision-encoder layers; 3 models (LLaVA-1.5-7B, Cambrian-8B, Molmo-7B-D), POPE + MME only, baselines = regular decoding and VCD, no human annotation, no GPU stated. IBD contrasts a standard and an 'image-biased' model with no extra training data and appeared in CVPR 2025 workshop proceedings (pp. 1624-1633).
    why: Defines the floor of a credible mitigation note: 3 models, 2 benchmarks, 1-2 baselines, no training. A search summary attributed LayerCD to the NeurIPS 2025 MAR workshop, but MAR's accepted list does not show it, so treat it as arXiv-only.
    code/data: https://github.com/maifoundations/LayerCD (MIT); IBD code none found

[6] HALLUCINOGEN: Benchmarking Hallucination in Implicit Reasoning within LVLMs (2025; 2nd Workshop on Uncertainty-Aware NLP (UncertaiNLP 2025) at EMNLP 2025, Suzhou) verified=True
    url: https://aclanthology.org/2025.uncertainlp-main.10/
    what: VQA benchmark that uses contextual-reasoning prompts as 'hallucination attacks' on salient vs latent objects; evaluates 11 LVLMs (incl. LLaMA-3.2, DeepSeek, Gemini) and two mitigation strategies; no human annotation stated; medical extension included.
    why: Shows an NLP workshop accepts a benchmark-only contribution built from prompt design over existing images, with no training. UncertaiNLP is a recurring, well-matched venue (it also hosted SHROOM-Visions 2026).
    code/data: not listed on the Anthology page (arXiv 2412.20622 exists)

[7] Defining and Quantifying Visual Hallucinations in VLMs (VHILT) (2025; 5th Workshop on Trustworthy NLP (TrustNLP 2025) at NAACL 2025) verified=True
    url: https://aclanthology.org/2025.trustnlp-main.32/
    what: Taxonomy of eight visual-hallucination types, a 2,000-sample captioning dataset with human category annotations, a VHVI vulnerability index, and evaluation of 8 VLMs. Only an 11-row subset is public on HF (vr25/vhil, Apache-2.0).
    why: Human-annotated taxonomy + 2k samples + 8 models was enough for TrustNLP; the tiny public release is a cautionary example of the data-availability weakness reviewers flag.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/vr25/vhil (Apache-2.0, 11 rows)

[8] SHROOM-Visions 2026 shared task overview (2026; Shared task at UncertaiNLP 2026, co-located with EMNLP 2026 (Budapest, Oct 24-29 2026)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25662
    what: Fourth SHROOM edition: span-level detection and 5-class classification of hallucinations in VQA/captioning outputs across four languages, built on the SHEEP dataset; 27 teams, 600+ submissions; top average scores 0.58 (char-level correlation), 0.46 (label-conditioned), 0.51 (IoU).
    why: Shared-task system papers are the lowest-barrier publication route for a student, and the labeled data is a ready-made test bed for an LLM-Check-style internal-signal detector. The 2026 edition is over; a 2027 edition is unverified (SemEval-2027's task list has no hallucination task).
    code/data: SHEEP dataset per the paper (not fetched)

[9] GHOST: Hallucination-Inducing Image Generation for Multimodal LLMs (2026; ICLR 2026 (per repo README); arXiv Sept 2025) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25178
    what: Optimises image embeddings to mislead an MLLM while keeping the target object absent, then conditions a diffusion model to render natural images; >28% hallucination success vs ~1% for data-driven baselines; images optimised on Qwen2.5-VL transfer to GPT-4o at 66.5%; human evaluation confirms object absence; fine-tuning on GHOST images reduces hallucination.
    why: State of the art for 'synthetic images that induce object hallucination' and a model of what a top-venue synthetic-data paper needs (human verification of absence, cross-model transfer). Too heavy to replicate, but its images, if released, are a free evaluation set.
    code/data: https://github.com/sudoparsa/GHOST (MIT; README says code/instructions 'will be updated soon')

[10] Synthetic benchmark canon: AutoHallusion and VHTest (2024; AutoHallusion: Findings of EMNLP 2024; VHTest: Findings of ACL 2024 (arXiv 2402.14683)) verified=True
    url: https://aclanthology.org/2024.findings-emnlp.493/
    what: AutoHallusion auto-builds cases via abnormal-object insertion, paired insertion and correlated-object removal (DALL-E + OWL-ViT), 3,129 QA pairs, 97.7%/98.7% attack success on synthetic/real images against GPT-4V, Gemini Pro Vision, Claude 3, LLaVA-1.5. VHTest generates 1,200 instances in 8 modes with DALL-E-3 (images released), tested on GPT-4V, LLaVA-1.5, MiniGPT-v2.
    why: Must-cite synthetic-data benchmarks; both depend on paid APIs for generation and evaluation, which is exactly the cost you must cap or replace with open local inpainting/diffusion.
    code/data: https://github.com/wuxiyang1996/AutoHallusion (BSD-3); https://github.com/wenhuang2000/VHTest (Apache-2.0)

[11] Canonical benchmark + baseline stack: POPE, AMBER, HallusionBench, VCD, OPERA (2023-2024; POPE EMNLP 2023; AMBER arXiv 2023; HallusionBench CVPR 2024; VCD and OPERA CVPR 2024) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.10355
    what: POPE: yes/no polling with random/popular/adversarial negatives (default 500 images x 3 negatives per dataset), reports Accuracy/P/R/F1 and Yes-ratio; lmms-lab/POPE mirror has 9,000 rows for lmms-eval. AMBER: LLM-free, 1,004 generative images + 14,216 discriminative questions over existence/attribute/relation, metrics CHAIR/Cover/Hal/Cog. HallusionBench: 346 images, 1,129 human-crafted questions, 15 models. VCD and OPERA: training-free decoding baselines with released code.
    why: Every paper must report these; POPE's Yes-ratio is the standard yes-bias control and VCD/OPERA are the baselines reviewers expect for any mitigation claim.
    code/data: https://github.com/RUCAIBox/POPE (MIT); https://github.com/junyangwang0410/AMBER (Apache-2.0); https://github.com/tianyi-lab/HallusionBench; https://huggingface.co/datasets/lmms-lab/POPE; https://github.com/shikiw/OPERA

[12] Surveys with benchmark critiques: Bai et al. 2024 and the 2025 evaluation/detection survey (2024-2025; Bai et al. arXiv 2024; 'A Survey of Multimodal Hallucination Evaluation and Detection' arXiv 2507.19024 (2025)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.18930
    what: Bai et al.: CHAIR limited to 80 COCO classes and sensitive to instruction/caption length (as POPE noted); THRONE's finding that discriminative scores do not predict generative hallucination; closed GPT judges hurt reproducibility. 2025 survey: GPT-4V and Qwen-VL favour 'No' on POPE-style tasks; coarse metrics; data-leakage risk in static benchmarks; most benchmarks single-task; LLM-judge standardisation lacking.
    why: Citable statements of the reviewer criticisms you must pre-empt (yes/no bias, length dependence, contamination, judge reliability). OpenReview review text could not be read (bot challenge), so these surveys plus the two analysis papers above are the evidence base for Part C.
    code/data: none

[13] Internal-signal detectors bridging LLM-Check to LVLMs: GLSim, VisER, Instruction Lens Score (2025-2026; GLSim NeurIPS 2025; VisER EMNLP 2026 main (arXiv 2608.30480); InsLen ICML 2026 (arXiv 2605.12258)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19972
    what: Training-free object-hallucination detectors from internal signals: GLSim uses global + local image-text embedding similarity; VisER scores whether support for each object mention comes from object-specific visual evidence vs the text prefix; InsLen uses instruction-token embeddings. All report AUROC/AUPR across multiple LVLMs.
    why: These are the multimodal counterparts of LLM-Check's hidden-state/attention/logit detectors; they are the baselines for any detection contribution and show the sub-field is live at top venues through 2026, so a small-model, low-compute replication/analysis is a defensible niche.
    code/data: https://github.com/Fraserlairh/Instruction-Lens-Score (license not checked); GLSim/VisER code not found on abs pages

-- gaps_and_opportunities --
  * No paper yet jointly measures mitigation methods under a POPE Yes-ratio AND informativeness (object recall/coverage) on both real COCO images and controlled edited images (LaMa-removed objects, ROHE recipe). A 'does the method look harder, or just say no more often?' study with 3 open models and 3-4 training-free decoders is open and cheap.
  * Abstention is absent from object-hallucination benchmarks: POPE forces yes/no. HalluLens' NonExistentRefusal task and Kalai et al.'s abstention-reward argument have no multimodal analogue; a small benchmark that allows 'cannot determine' and scores calibrated abstention is open and needs only prompt design over existing POPE/AMBER/ROHE-style images.
  * Internal-signal detection (GLSim, VisER, InsLen) is reported only on 7B+ models; nobody reports how these detectors and decoding-time mitigations interact, or how they behave on 2-3B models that fit a 6GB card. A small-model replication with yes-bias analysis is a defensible niche.
  * Synthetic hallucination-inducing images (GHOST, AutoHallusion, VHTest) depend on paid APIs or heavy optimisation; whether open local inpainting/diffusion edits elicit the same hallucination rates as those API-made images, object for object, is untested and matches the project title exactly.
  * HOPE shows POPE is saturated for 2025 models; a 'Yes-ratio vs distractor type' update with 2026 open models (Qwen2.5-VL-3B, InternVL3-2B) is an easy, citable refresh.
  * SHROOM-Visions 2026 released span-labelled hallucination data for LVLM outputs; no verified work tests hidden-state/attention detectors (LLM-Check style) on it at small scale.

-- recommended_for_this_project --
  * Choose the analysis/benchmark shape, not training: replicate the structure of 'Does Playing it Safe Count as Faithfulness?' at smaller scale: 3 open models from at least 2 families, 3-4 training-free decoders (greedy, VCD, OPERA, LayerCD), POPE with Yes-ratio, AMBER (CHAIR+Cover+Hal via the official LLM-free scorer), plus a 300-500-image edited-real set you build with LaMa (ROHE recipe) and verify yourself. Report precision, recall, Yes-ratio and informativeness together.
  * Make it unique by adding the abstention angle from the advisor's list: give POPE-style prompts a third 'cannot determine' option, score with a HalluLens-style refusal metric, and frame the result through Kalai et al. (evaluations reward guessing) and Xu et al. (inevitability). That is a bridge nobody has published in the object-hallucination setting.
  * Tooling: use lmms-eval with lmms-lab/POPE, AMBER's inference.py, and the official VCD/OPERA/LayerCD repos as baselines. Avoid GPT-4-judged metrics except a capped 100-sample spot check (cost and the reproducibility criticism).
  * Compute plan: pilot on the GTX 1060 with Qwen2-VL-2B (Apache-2.0, ~2-5GB per third-party guides) in 8-bit/4-bit; LLaVA-1.5-7B 4-bit needs 'less than 8GB' per its README, so it is borderline on 6GB and the 7B sweeps belong on a rented RTX 4090 or A6000/A40. Cost arithmetic from RunPod's official page (4090 $0.89/hr, A6000/A40 $0.59/hr, A100-80GB $1.79/hr): assuming ~3 GPU-hours per model x decoder sweep, 3 models x 4 decoders = ~36 GPU-hours = about $21-32, with a 2x margin $40-65; on an A100 about $65-130. Measure real throughput in a 1-hour pilot before committing.
  * Venue plan: write the RES201 report in ACL short-paper format (4 pages + appendix) so it can go directly, non-archival, to EACL 2027 SRW (Dec 15 2026; mentorship request by Nov 6 2026) or NAACL 2027 SRW (Jan 11 2027; mentorship by Nov 16 2026); submit the fuller report to JURI in parallel; if results are strong, target an ICLR 2027 workshop (~Feb 1 2027, list out Nov 29 2026) or a CVPR 2027 workshop (~Mar 2027). Skip EMNLP 2026, NeurIPS 2026 and ICLR 2027 main (deadlines passed).
  * Cite the canon: CHAIR (Rohrbach et al. 2018), POPE, AMBER, HallusionBench, VCD, OPERA, Bai et al. survey, AutoHallusion, VHTest, HOPE, GHOST, ROHE; cite 'Playing it Safe', SumGD (fixed-length CHAIR) and the 2025 evaluation survey when defending metric choices.
  * Avoid: training-based methods (oDPO/HA-DPO need A100-class GPUs), building a benchmark from paid API image generation at scale, a single model family, and CHAIR-only claims without length control (fix max tokens at 64/128/256/512 as SumGD does).
  * Human validation: self-annotate the edited images for object absence (ROHE verified 5,504 units), have a teammate or the advisor double-check a 50-100 sample and report agreement; reviewers ask for this on every benchmark paper.

-- cautions --
  * OpenReview review text (weaknesses sections) could not be read: both forum pages and the API redirect to a bot challenge. Part C criticisms come from surveys and recent analysis papers, not verbatim reviews.
  * The WebSearch budget ran out before confirming: SHROOM-Visions/SemEval 2027 (SemEval-2027's task list shows no hallucination task), AAAI-27 student track deadlines, Google Colab pricing, ICCAIS 2026/2027 status, ICAIII 2027, AIIT 2027 details, NeurIPS 2026 workshop deadlines (almost certainly passed), and TrustNLP/UncertaiNLP 2027 editions.
  * LayerCD's attribution to the NeurIPS 2025 MAR workshop came from a search summary and is contradicted by MAR 2025's accepted-paper list; treat it as arXiv-only.
  * CVPR 2027 location/dates (Seattle, Jun 20-25 2027) come from the CVPR call-for-workshops page and aggregators; the main CFP page did not display them.
  * EACL 2027 SRW lists its ARR commitment deadline as 'December 22, 2027', almost certainly a typo for 2026; the ICLR 2027 call-for-workshops page says workshops are 'April 29 and 30, 2026', also a typo (ICLR 2027 runs Apr 26-30 2027). Confirm with organisers.
  * RunPod prices are from the official pricing page on 2026-10-12; a third-party blog listed a cheaper community-cloud 4090 tier ($0.34/hr) that was not verified, and Vast.ai/Lambda figures came only from blogs.
  * GTX 1060 feasibility: the LLaVA README states 4-bit 7B needs 'less than 8GB', so 7B is likely out of memory on 6GB; bitsandbytes 4-bit kernels on Pascal can be slow or unsupported (unverified). Qwen2-VL-2B VRAM figures are from third-party guides, not the model card.
  * The GPU-hour estimate is arithmetic from stated prices and an assumed ~3 GPU-hours per model x method sweep; the throughput assumption is not from a source.
  * VHILT's public HF subset has only 11 rows and the 2,000-sample set was 'pending acceptance'; GHOST's README says code/instructions 'will be updated soon' and may not ship images; HOPE's repo shows no license. Check availability and licensing before building on any of them.
  * The lmms-lab/POPE mirror carries COCO images; COCO's own licence terms apply to redistribution.
  * ICCAIS could not be verified at all (iccais.tech unreachable); the only trace is an aggregator listing a 7th edition in Dubai, April 2024.

-- confidence_notes -- High confidence for venue dates read from official pages (EACL/NAACL SRW, ACL 2027, ICLR 2027, CVPR 2027 CFP, EMNLP 2026, RunPod pricing). Medium for per-paper compute and dataset sizes: most come from full-text or PDF extraction, but abstracts rarely state GPUs (HOPE, LayerCD, HALLUCINOGEN, IBD give none). Low for regional Saudi/Gulf IEEE conferences (several sites unreachable or aggregator-only) and for cheaper cloud tiers. Primary sources were opened for every item marked verified; the WebSearch cap was hit near the end, so the last checks (SemEval, AAAI, Colab, ICCAIS) rely on WebFetch only or remain open.

######## CRITIC ########

GAP: Input resolution and visual-token budget as an uncontrolled confound (plus harness support on Pascal)
  why: Every agent tells the student to cap max_pixels to roughly 256-640 visual tokens and run 4-bit to fit the GTX 1060, and SID (ICLR 2025) shows that deliberately dropping vision tokens induces object hallucination; GHOST-Q covers the quantization factor but no agent covers resolution or token count. Numbers produced at a non-standard resolution are incomparable to vendor and leaderboard tables, and square 512/1024 px generated images versus 640x480 COCO photos may receive different token budgets under dynamic-resolution models, which would confound the central real-vs-synthetic comparison. Nobody confirmed that lmms-eval or VLMEvalKit can load 4-bit or GGUF models on an sm_61 GPU pinned to PyTorch 2.14.
  prompt: Search 2024-2026 papers and tool documentation for how input resolution and the number of visual tokens change object-hallucination scores in LVLMs: Qwen2-VL/Qwen2.5-VL/Qwen3-VL dynamic resolution (min_pixels/max_pixels), InternVL tiling (max_num), token-pruning work (FastV, SparseVLM, PyramidDrop, SID's CT2S) and any paper that reports POPE, CHAIR or AMBER as a function of resolution or token budget. Find the default image-size and generation settings that VLMEvalKit and lmms-eval apply to POPE, AMBER, HallusionBench and MME for these model families, and whether either harness can load bitsandbytes 4-bit or GGUF models on a Pascal sm_61 GPU with PyTorch 2.14 cu126 wheels. Check whether square generated images (512x512 or 1024x1024 from SD/FLUX) and COCO photos (mostly 640x480) yield different visual-token counts under dynamic-resolution models and whether any synthetic-vs-real benchmark (ODE, AutoHallusion, Causal-HalBench, AIGCs Confuse AI Too) controlled for that. Recommend a fixed-resolution, fixed-max_new_tokens protocol with exact flags that keeps the student's numbers comparable to published tables, and give URLs for every claim.

GAP: Cross-lingual (Arabic) object hallucination: an unclaimed, regionally relevant angle
  why: The student asked for a unique contribution and is at KFUPM, yet none of the seven agents mentions multilingual or Arabic hallucination; SHROOM-Visions 2026 covers only EN/FR/IT/ZH. Prompt language is a free experimental factor on the same images and models, it maps directly onto the advisor's 'language prior beats visual evidence' framing (a lower-resource prompt language should strengthen the prior), and translating the short yes/no questions of POPE and AMBER costs almost nothing. Without this check the strategy may miss the cheapest route to novelty and to a regional venue.
  prompt: Search for 2024-2026 work on multilingual and Arabic hallucination in vision-language models, starting from 'Mitigating Multilingual Hallucination in Large Vision-Language Models' (Qu et al. 2024), ALM-Bench (CVPR 2025, includes Arabic), CVQA (NeurIPS 2024), CAMEL-Bench, AIN (MBZUAI 2025), Peacock (ACL 2024) and Dallah, verifying each on arXiv or GitHub. Determine whether any paper measures object-existence hallucination (yes-ratio, POPE F1, CHAIR) as a function of prompt language on the same images, and whether translated versions of POPE, AMBER, HallusionBench or MME exist on Hugging Face or in VLMEvalKit's multilingual sets. Check the model cards of Qwen3-VL-2B/4B, Qwen3.5-2B/4B, Gemma 4 E2B/E4B, InternVL3.5-2B and LFM2.5-VL-3B for stated Arabic support. Estimate the cost of translating the roughly 3,000-14,000 short yes/no questions in POPE and AMBER with a local MT model or a batch API, and describe a cheap validation protocol (back-translation plus a native-speaker check of a sample). Conclude whether an Arabic-vs-English object-hallucination comparison on real and synthetic images would be novel, and give URLs and licenses.

GAP: Statistical validity: error bars, paired tests and item-efficient subsets for a small benchmark run on a weak GPU
  why: The benchmarks agent cites HQM's finding that POPE's parallel-forms reliability is only 0.36 (a 500-item subset can reorder models) and mentions McNemar once, but no agent explains how to report confidence intervals, test the matched real-vs-synthetic difference, size the human-verification sample, or pick a small representative item subset so the 1060 can cover more models and methods. A conference reviewer in 2026 will ask for all four, and the compute limits make subset selection the difference between 2 and 6 evaluated models.
  prompt: Search for 2024-2026 methodology on statistically sound evaluation of LLM and VLM benchmarks: 'Adding Error Bars to Evals' (Miller 2024), tinyBenchmarks (ICML 2024) and other IRT or anchor-point subset-selection methods, paired bootstrap and clustered standard errors over images, McNemar or paired permutation tests for matched real-vs-synthetic items, and the HQM test-retest and parallel-forms protocol (arXiv 2406.17115). Report whether any object-hallucination paper reports confidence intervals or significance tests, and whether tinyBenchmarks-style subset selection has been applied to POPE, AMBER or HallusionBench so that a weak GPU can evaluate more models on fewer items. Compute or cite the number of paired yes/no items needed to detect a 3-5 point difference in accuracy or yes-ratio at 80 percent power. Collect the inter-annotator agreement conventions used by recent hallucination benchmarks (VHTest kappa 0.958, ReactBench two-stage review, GHOST human absence check) and the sample sizes they verified. Give concrete recommended sample sizes, tests and Python libraries with URLs.

GAP: Where the hallucination originates: encoder probes, CLIP-blind pairs (MMVP), self-grounding checks, and the missing bridge to Xu et al.
  why: The canonical 'Eyes Wide Shut / MMVP' paper (CVPR 2024), which shows CLIP-blind image pairs drive MLLM failures, is absent from all seven outputs even though it decides whether synthetic images fool the encoder or the language model; DO-Bench's prior-vs-evidence axes and HALP probes are cited but no agent proposes the cheap encoder-level probe or the model's own grounding output (Qwen bounding boxes, Moondream point) as a verifier, both of which run on a 6 GB GPU. The agents bridge Kalai et al. but not Xu et al.'s inevitability result, the other theory paper on the advisor's list.
  prompt: Search for 2024-2026 work that attributes object hallucination to the vision encoder versus the language model: 'Eyes Wide Shut? Exploring the Visual Shortcomings of Multimodal LLMs' (MMVP, CLIP-blind pairs, CVPR 2024), linear probes on frozen CLIP or SigLIP features for object presence, text-only 'blind' baselines that answer POPE without the image (VDD/M3ID style), and papers measuring whether diffusion-generated or inpainted images shift CLIP/SigLIP embeddings (ODE's CLIP-space comparison, SalArt-VQA). Also find work that uses a VLM's own grounding output (Qwen2.5-VL/Qwen3-VL bounding boxes, Moondream point/detect, Ferret, Shikra) to verify the objects it names, i.e. self-grounding consistency as a hallucination detector, and whether it was tested on models under 4B parameters. Finally, check whether any 2024-2026 paper extends the formal 'Hallucination is Inevitable' result (Xu et al. 2024) or Kalai et al.'s calibration bound to conditional or grounded generation such as captioning or VQA, so the report can cite a theoretical bridge rather than assert one. Report arXiv IDs, code links and the cheapest version of each experiment that runs on a 6 GB GPU.

-- contradictions --
  * RunPod prices: mitigation and detection_transfer quote a two-tier table (RTX 4090 $0.34 community / $0.74 secure; RTX 3090 $0.22/$0.50; A100 80GB $1.19-1.59; H100 PCIe $1.99/$2.89), whereas compute, synthetic and venues quote a single-tier table (RTX 4090 $0.89, RTX 3090 $0.50, A40/L4/A6000 $0.59, A5000 $0.27, A100 80GB $1.79, H100 $2.89-3.99) and build their dollar budgets on it. My fetch of runpod.io/pricing ('Updated September 27, 2026') shows the two-tier table: 4090 $0.34/$0.74, 3090 $0.22/$0.50, A5000 $0.16/$0.27, A40 $0.35/$0.49, A6000 $0.33/$0.53, L4 $0.44/$0.49, L40S $0.79/$1.09, A100 PCIe $1.19/$1.59, A100 SXM $1.39/$1.59, H100 PCIe $1.99/$2.89. The $0.89 and $1.79 figures are not on the page, so the compute/venues/synthetic budgets are roughly 2x conservative (an upper bound, not an error in direction).
  * AutoHallusion venue: benchmarks says 'EMNLP 2024 (per repository; main vs Findings not verified)' and synthetic says 'EMNLP 2024 main (UMD publication page)', while venues says 'Findings of EMNLP 2024'. The ACL Anthology page 2024.findings-emnlp.493 (fetched) confirms Findings of EMNLP 2024; cite it that way.
  * LLaVA-1.5-7B on the GTX 1060: compute calls it feasible in NF4 (~5.5 GB estimate) for POPE/CHAIR at batch 1 with max_new_tokens <= 128; mitigation calls it borderline and recommends cloud-only; venues says it is likely out of memory because the LLaVA README states 4-bit needs 'less than 8GB'. Unresolved by any source; the strategy should not rely on a local 7B run until a day-one measurement, and the three agents' 'fits/does not fit' statements should be reconciled by that measurement.
  * Model generation for the core set: latest recommends the 2026 natively-multimodal small models (Qwen3.5-2B/4B, Gemma 4 E2B/E4B, LFM2.5-VL-3B, MiniCPM-V-4.6) as the headline set; mitigation and detection_transfer recommend Qwen2-VL-2B / Qwen2.5-VL-3B / Qwen3-VL-2B / SmolVLM because the mitigation code (VCD, PAI, Nullu, MaskCD) and the only <=3B aligned checkpoints (SENTINEL Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct and REVERSE Qwen2.5-VL-3B, both confirmed in the repos' READMEs) target them; compute warns that Qwen3.5 and Gemma 4 need transformers-from-source that may pull PyTorch > 2.14, and the PyTorch dev-discuss notice (confirmed, 4 Sept 2026) makes 2.14 the last release with Pascal wheels. The strategy must choose or split: 2026 models for the benchmark table, 2024-25 models for mitigation ports.
  * Qwen3.5-4B HallusionBench: latest reports vendor 65.0 '(thinking)' versus 51.7 in 'Liquid's table' and builds a 'protocol gap' finding on it. The Qwen3.5-4B card (fetched) lists 65.0 without stating thinking or non-thinking mode, and the LFM2.5-VL-3B card (fetched) carries a POPE column (88.7 / 84.0 / 86.9 / 88.9 / 86.0 for LFM2.5-VL-3B / Gemma4 E2B / E4B / InternVL3.5-4B / Qwen3.5-4B) but no HallusionBench column, so the 51.7 is unverified. Separately, compute marked the Qwen/Qwen3.5-2B id as 'inferred, verify' while latest treated it as verified; the page exists (Apache-2.0, image input, Feb 2026, HallusionBench 58.0 thinking / 51.3 non-thinking), resolving that one in favor of latest.
  * Direction of the synthetic-vs-real effect (a literature-level conflict that the agents weight differently): benchmarks and detection_transfer lean on HaloQuest (models slightly better on synthetic images, r=0.97 with real) and AutoHallusion (GPT-4V 68.5% synthetic vs 62.9% real) to justify synthetic data as a proxy, while synthetic and the ODE item argue the opposite from 'AIGCs Confuse AI Too' (more hallucination on SD1.5 regenerations, up to 5.63 points on POPE-random) and ODE (more hallucination on generated or fresh images than on COCO, attributed to contamination). The strategy must state this as an open hypothesis with both outcomes publishable and must separate a 'provenance effect' from a 'contamination effect' (fresh real photos as a third arm).
  * Decoding-overhead attribution: mitigation credits DeGF (ICLR 2025) with the VCD 2.01x / OPERA ~7x / HALC 6.51x latency measurements; compute credits 'arXiv 2605.24024 Table 6' (greedy 61.3 ms/step, VCD 123.2 ms, OPERA 435.7 ms) for the same 2.01x / 7.12x ratios and cites that ID without a title. One of the two is a re-citation; confirm the identity of 2605.24024 before citing it.
  * GTX 1060 fp16 behaviour: compute says fp16 runs at roughly fp32 speed on Pascal (memory benefit only), detection_transfer says Pascal has 'very slow fp16'. Both agree there is no fp16 speed-up, but they imply different throughputs; neither is measured, and the compute agent's images-per-minute table depends on which is true.

-- suspicious_claims --
  * VERIFIED (Crossref API, DOI 10.1038/s41586-026-10549-w): Kalai, Nachum, Vempala, Zhang, 'Evaluating large language models for accuracy incentivizes hallucinations', Nature 653(8116):1047-1051, online 22 Apr 2026, print 28 May 2026. The latest agent's must-cite claim is correct; cite it alongside arXiv 2509.04664.
  * VERIFIED (Hugging Face pages): Qwen/Qwen3.5-2B and Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B exist (Apache-2.0, February 2026, image input; 2B HallusionBench 58.0 thinking / 51.3 non-thinking; 4B HallusionBench 65.0, MMBench 89.4, MMMU 77.6 with the mode not stated). The Gemma 4 launch blog (huggingface.co/blog/gemma4, 2 Apr 2026) exists and announces E2B (2.3B effective), E4B (4.5B effective), 12B, 31B and 26B-A4B under Apache 2 with image, text and audio input. Neither family is a hallucinated model id.
  * VERIFIED (dev-discuss.pytorch.org thread 3432, posted 4 Sept 2026 by atalman): PyTorch 2.14 is the last release that ships CUDA 12.6 wheels and 2.15 drops Maxwell/Pascal/Volta, so the student must pin torch==2.14 from the cu126 index (or build from source) on the GTX 1060. This is the single most important feasibility fact in the seven outputs and it holds.

(Fazli et al.; 1 Sept 2026; abstract confirms that lower hallucination comes with lower object recall/coverage but does not name the six methods), 2511.03768 Common-O (Ross et al.; NeurIPS D&B 2025; best model 35%, 1% on complex), 2406.17115 HQM/HQH (Yan et al.; v3 25 Feb 2026), 2603.19765 FREAK (Yin et al.; 20 Mar 2026), 2609.29999 GHOST-Q (Rehman, Shafique; 24 Sept 2026; ICASSP 2027 submission; 10 of 36 paired effects significant) all exist with the stated titles. ######## ANGLE: FILL: Input resolution and visual-token budget as an uncontrolled confound (plus harness support on Pascal) ########
angle: Input resolution and visual-token budget as an uncontrolled confound in object-hallucination benchmarking (Qwen2/2.5/3-VL dynamic resolution, InternVL tiling, token pruning), the default image/generation settings that VLMEvalKit and lmms-eval silently apply to POPE/AMBER/HallusionBench/MME, whether synthetic-vs-real benchmarks controlled for it, and what actually runs on a GTX 1060 (sm_61) with PyTorch cu126 wheels.

[1] Qwen2-VL / Qwen2.5-VL dynamic resolution: 28 px per visual token, min_pixels/max_pixels, and the Table 7 token-budget ablation (2024-2025; HF model card + Qwen2-VL technical report (arXiv 2409.12191) + transformers source) verified=True
    url: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct
    what: Each visual token covers a 28x28 px block (14-px ViT patches merged 2x2); smart_resize rounds H and W to multiples of 28 and rescales only when the pixel count leaves [min_pixels, max_pixels] (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/huggingface/transformers/main/src/transformers/models/qwen2_vl/image_processing_qwen2_vl.py). The shipped preprocessor_config.json sets min_pixels=3136 and max_pixels=12,845,056 (4-16,384 tokens; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct/raw/main/preprocessor_config.json), while the transformers class default is max_pixels=28*28*1280=1,003,520; the card recommends a 256-1280-token window and exact control via resized_height/resized_width (rounded to multiples of 28). Qwen2-VL report Table 7 (https://arxiv.org/html/2409.12191): fixed 64/576/1600/3136 tokens give InfoVQA 28.85/65.72/74.99/77.27, RealWorldQA 56.47/65.88/69.54/70.59, OCRBench 572/828/824/786, MMMU 53.33/52.78/52.89/53.44 versus dynamic (avg 1,924 tokens) 75.89/70.07/866/53.44; the paper evaluated with min_pixels=100x28x28 and warns that 'excessive enlargement causes these images to deviate from the training data distribution'.
    why: Computed from the quoted formula: a 640x480 COCO photo becomes 644x476 = 23x17 = 391 tokens, a 512x512 SD image 504x504 = 324 tokens, and a 1024x1024 FLUX/SDXL image 1036x1036 = 1,369 tokens (1,225 under the 1,003,520 class default), so a real-vs-synthetic comparison silently varies the token budget up to 4.2x unless image sizes are matched. Table 7 shows the budget moves scores by tens of points, and it contains no hallucination benchmark, which is itself an opening.
    code/data: Weights: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct and -7B-Instruct (license not re-checked this session); processor code in transformers (Apache-2.0)

[2] Qwen3-VL preprocessor: 32 px per token (patch 16, merge 2) and the harnesses' Qwen3-VL settings (2025; HF config + lmms-eval and VLMEvalKit source) verified=True
    url: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct/raw/main/preprocessor_config.json
    what: Qwen3-VL ships patch_size=16, merge_size=2 (so one token = 32x32 px) with size.shortest_edge=65,536 and longest_edge=16,777,216 pixels (64-16,384 tokens), image_processor_type Qwen2VLImageProcessorFast. lmms-eval's simple/qwen3_vl.py defaults to min_pixels=256*32*32, max_pixels=2048*32*32, bf16, greedy, max_new_tokens 128 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/EvolvingLMMs-Lab/lmms-eval/main/lmms_eval/models/simple/qwen3_vl.py). VLMEvalKit's Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct entry runs through vLLM with sampling: temperature=0.7, top_p=0.8, top_k=20, presence_penalty=1.5, max_new_tokens=16384 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit/main/vlmeval/config.py).
    why: At identical pixel sizes the token count changes across model generations (640x480 -> 20x15 = 300 tokens, 512x512 -> 256, 1024x1024 -> 1,024 under Qwen3-VL versus 391/324/1,369 under Qwen2.5-VL), so 'same resolution' is not 'same budget' across families; the OpenCompass Qwen3-VL numbers are sampled (non-deterministic) and produced by vLLM, which does not run on Pascal, so they cannot be reproduced on the student's hardware.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct (license not re-checked); no Qwen3-VL GGUF listed in llama.cpp multimodal docs

[3] InternVL dynamic tiling (448x448 tiles, 256 tokens per tile, max_num) and the real-vs-square token gap it creates (2024-2025; HF model card (InternVL3) + InternVL2.5 report (arXiv 2412.05271)) verified=True
    url: https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3-8B
    what: find_closest_aspect_ratio picks the (i,j) grid with i*j <= max_num closest to the image aspect ratio and only moves to a bigger grid on an exact tie when area > 0.5*448*448*i*j; dynamic_preprocess crops the resized image into i*j tiles and appends a 448x448 thumbnail whenever blocks != 1 (code quoted from https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3-8B/raw/main/README.md). The InternVL2.5 report states 'a 448x448 image tile is represented by 256 visual tokens' after pixel unshuffle (1024 -> 256) (https://arxiv.org/html/2412.05271). The HF example uses max_num=12; lmms-eval simple/internvl.py defaults max_num=12 (bf16 hardcoded, max_new_tokens 1024 greedy); VLMEvalKit's InternVLChat uses max_num=6 for POPE/AMBER/HallusionBench/MME because none of them is in its res_12/18/24 lists (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit/main/vlmeval/vlm/internvl/internvl_chat.py).
    why: Derived from that code: a 640x480 COCO photo -> 4x3 grid + thumbnail = 13 tiles = 3,328 tokens (max_num=12) or 3x2 + thumbnail = 1,792 tokens (max_num=6); a 512x512 generated image -> 1 tile = 256 tokens with no thumbnail; a 1024x1024 image -> 2,560 (max_num=12) or 1,280 (max_num=6).
