That is a 7-13x token-budget gap between the real and square-synthetic halves of the student's central comparison, plus a 2x gap between the two harnesses' defaults, before any model property is measured.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3-2B and InternVL3-1B (small enough for 6 GB in 4-bit/GGUF); code in model card README

[4] VLMEvalKit defaults for POPE/AMBER/HallusionBench/MME (Qwen2-VL/2.5-VL and InternVL wrappers) (2024-2026 (main branch); OpenCompass VLMEvalKit source (config.py, vlm/qwen2_vl/model.py, vlm/qwen2_vl/prompt.py, dataset/image_yorn.py, vlm/internvl/internvl_chat.py)) verified=True
    url: https://github.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit/blob/main/vlmeval/config.py
    what: Qwen2-VL-2B/7B and Qwen2.5-VL-3B/7B (and their -AWQ) entries fix min_pixels=1280*28*28 (1,003,520 px) and max_pixels=16384*28*28; Qwen2VLChat defaults max_new_tokens=2048, top_p=0.001, top_k=1, temperature=0.01, do_sample=True, torch_dtype='auto', and attn_implementation='flash_attention_2' is hardcoded with no fallback; per-image min/max/total_pixels are attached in _prepare_content and resolved by qwen_vl_utils.process_vision_info. InternVLChat: bf16, do_sample=False, max_new_tokens=4096, default max_num=6, optional load_in_8bit. Prompts: ImageYORNDataset covers MME/HallusionBench/POPE/AMBER/VSR; AMBER gets '\nPlease answer yes or no.' appended by the dataset; Qwen2VLPromptMixin appends ' Please answer yes or no.' for POPE/HallusionBench only when use_custom_prompt=True (the Qwen2.5-VL entries set it False, so the raw question is sent); InternVL appends 'Answer the question using a single word or phrase' for MME/HallusionBench; Y/N answers go through YOrN_Extraction with an LLM-judge fallback when 'Unknown'.
    why: The min_pixels floor upsamples small images: 640x480 -> 1176x868 = 1,302 tokens and 512x512 -> 1008x1008 = 1,296 tokens (computed from smart_resize), so OpenCompass leaderboard POPE/HallusionBench numbers for Qwen2.5-VL were produced at ~1,300 tokens, not at native 391/324, and coincidentally with near-equal budgets for real and square images. Reproducing them on a GTX 1060 requires patching flash_attention_2 -> sdpa and the dtype, and the 'do_sample=True, temperature=0.01' setting must be stated because it is not strictly greedy.
    code/data: https://github.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit (license not re-checked; widely used as Apache-2.0)

[5] lmms-eval defaults for pope, hallusion_bench_image, mme, amber_g and the Qwen2.5-VL / InternVL wrappers (2024-2026 (main branch); lmms-eval source (tasks/pope, tasks/hallusion_bench, tasks/mme, tasks/amber_g, models/simple/qwen2_5_vl.py, models/simple/internvl.py)) verified=True
    url: https://github.com/EvolvingLMMs-Lab/lmms-eval/blob/main/lmms_eval/tasks/pope/pope.yaml
    what: pope.yaml: dataset lmms-lab-encoder/POPE, post_prompt '\nAnswer the question using a single word or phrase.', max_new_tokens 128, temperature 0, do_sample false, metrics accuracy/precision/recall/F1/yes_ratio. hallusion_bench_image.yaml: max_new_tokens 128, temperature 0, empty pre/post prompt (metrics aAcc/qAcc/fAcc). mme.yaml: max_new_tokens 16, same post-prompt. amber_g.yaml (Kyunnilee/amber_g): max_new_tokens 2048, temperature 0, 'until: []', metrics CHAIR/Cover/Hal/Cog. Model wrapper simple/qwen2_5_vl.py defaults min_pixels=256*28*28 (200,704), max_pixels=1,605,632 (2,048 tokens), attn_implementation None, batch_size 1, system prompt 'You are a helpful assistant.' (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/EvolvingLMMs-Lab/lmms-eval/main/lmms_eval/models/simple/qwen2_5_vl.py); simple/internvl.py: max_num=12, bf16 hardcoded, max_new_tokens 1024 greedy; no 4-bit/GGUF flag exists in these wrappers (only InternVL load_in_8bit).
    why: Under these bounds a 640x480 COCO image stays native (391 tokens), 512x512 stays 324 and 1024x1024 stays 1,369, so the same model evaluated on the same POPE image receives 391 tokens in lmms-eval but ~1,302 in VLMEvalKit (and 3,328 vs 1,792 for InternVL): the student must pick one harness, state it, and never mix the two in one table. The AMBER-generative cap of 2048 tokens versus OPERA-style 512 changes CHAIR materially (see OPERA item).
    code/data: https://github.com/EvolvingLMMs-Lab/lmms-eval (license not re-checked); datasets lmms-lab-encoder/POPE, lmms-lab-encoder/HallusionBench, lmms-lab-encoder/MME, Kyunnilee/amber_g on HF

[6] SID: Self-Introspective Decoding (CT2S shows that starving the decoder of vision tokens induces object hallucination) (2025; ICLR 2025) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.02032
    what: Context-and-Text-aware Token Selection keeps only the ~10% least important vision tokens after an early decoder layer (about layer 3; layer 5 for Q-Former models), which 'adaptively amplifies' hallucination; the amplified logits are then subtracted contrastively. Figure 6 shows that late-layer selection collapses back to normal decoding and that 0% retention loses visual grounding; Table 3 reports LLaVA-1.5 CHAIR_S 44.2 for SID versus 46.8 for VCD (greedy).
    why: Direct evidence that the number of vision tokens surviving inside the LLM is a hallucination knob: an aggressive max_pixels cut, a small InternVL max_num, or a pruning method does unintentionally what CT2S does on purpose. SID is also a training-free mitigation baseline that fits a 3B/7B model and can be compared across token budgets.
    code/data: https://github.com/huofushuo/SID (license not checked)

[7] Token-pruning reference curves: POPE versus retained visual tokens (SparseVLM, VisionZip, PyramidDrop; FastV reports none) (2024-2025; SparseVLM (ICML 2025); VisionZip (CVPR 2025, arXiv 2412.04467); PyramidDrop (arXiv 2410.17247); FastV (ECCV 2024, arXiv 2403.06764)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.04417
    what: SparseVLM's table for LLaVA-1.5-7B: POPE 85.9 at 576 tokens; at 192/128/64 retained tokens FastV 64.8/53.4/38.2, ToMe 72.4/62.8/52.5, PDrop 82.3/82.3/55.9, SparseVLM 85.3/85.0/77.5. VisionZip Table 12 (LLaVA-NeXT-7B): POPE 86.4 at 2,880 tokens -> 86.3 (640) -> 82.1 (320) -> 74.8 (160); LLaVA-1.5-7B 85.3 at 192 (https://arxiv.org/html/2412.04467). PyramidDrop: 85.9 -> 86.0 at lambda=0.5 (https://arxiv.org/html/2410.17247). FastV's own paper reports Nocaps/Flickr/A-OKVQA/MMMU etc. and no POPE or CHAIR, with heavy degradation at K=2,R=90% (https://arxiv.org/html/2403.06764).
    why: POPE is flat down to roughly a quarter to a third of the tokens and then falls off a cliff (up to -47 points), and where the cliff sits depends on which tokens are kept, so an unequal budget between real and synthetic sets, or a budget under ~200 tokens on LLaVA-class models, is a confound as large as the effects the student wants to measure. These are the published curves to cite and to re-plot for Qwen2.5-VL/InternVL3 at native versus capped resolution.
    code/data: https://github.com/Gumpest/SparseVLMs ; https://github.com/dvlab-research/VisionZip ; https://github.com/Cooperx521/PyramidDrop ; https://github.com/pkunlp-icler/FastV (licenses not checked)

[8] Why Y/N scores can hide the damage: Feather the Throttle, 'Are We Solving the Right Problem?', and object-size sensitivity (MLLMs Know Where to Look) (2025; ICCV 2025 (Endo et al.); ACL 2025 Findings (Wen et al., arXiv 2502.11501); ICLR 2025 (Zhang et al., arXiv 2502.17422)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.13180
    what: Endo et al. show that early-layer pruning removes 'most tokens towards the top of the image' yet keeps benchmark accuracy, while localization degrades more than 5x. Wen et al. ask why many pruning methods 'underperform even compared to naive random token selection' and whether 'current evaluation protocols [are] comprehensive and unbiased'. Zhang et al. find MLLM accuracy 'is very sensitive to the size of the visual subject' even though attention lands on the right region, and fix it with training-free crop/zoom (code https://github.com/saccharomycetes/mllms_know).
    why: POPE and MME are coarse Yes/No probes that can look stable while fine-grained grounding is destroyed, so 'resolution did not change POPE' is not evidence that resolution does not matter. Object size in pixels is the hidden variable linking resolution to object hallucination, which argues for stratifying POPE/AMBER items by COCO object area and pairing Y/N metrics with a generative metric (AMBER-g or CHAIR).
    code/data: https://github.com/saccharomycetes/mllms_know ; Feather/Wen code not checked

[9] Synthetic-image hallucination benchmarks and their (absent) resolution control: AIGCs Confuse AI Too, AutoHallusion, ODE (2024-2025; arXiv (Gao et al. 2024); AutoHallusion arXiv 2406.10900; ODE arXiv 2409.09318 (v2 July 2025)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.08542
    what: Gao et al. regenerate POPE (500 images) and AMBER (1,004 images, 15,200 annotations) with Stable Diffusion v1.5 and report that 'synthetic images induce higher hallucinations' (e.g., Qwen-VL POPE-random 86.07 natural vs 78.37 synthetic); the paper states no image-resolution or token-budget matching (https://arxiv.org/html/2403.08542). AutoHallusion generates scenes with DALL-E 3 ('all scene and edited images are 1024x1024 and inserted objects are 200x200'), edits with DALL-E 2 inpainting, and uses 126 COCO images 'with proper resizing to fit the input of image models' (https://arxiv.org/html/2406.10900, code https://github.com/wuxiyang1996/AutoHallusion). ODE builds open-set tests with FLUX.1-dev and SD 1.5, filters at a 0.65 confidence threshold, and never states a resolution (https://arxiv.org/html/2409.09318, code https://github.com/Iridescent-y/ODE).
    why: All three compare square generated images against 4:3 COCO photos without reporting the resulting visual-token counts, so part of the reported 'synthetic images hallucinate more' effect may be a token-budget or upscaling artifact (256 vs 1,792-3,328 tokens under InternVL; 324 vs 391 under native Qwen2.5-VL). A resolution-matched replication is a clean, cheap, publishable contribution that none of them made.
    code/data: https://github.com/wuxiyang1996/AutoHallusion ; https://github.com/Iridescent-y/ODE ; no code link found on the Gao et al. abstract page

[10] CHAIR depends on generation length: OPERA's 64- vs 512-token protocol and POPE's motivation (2023-2024; OPERA (CVPR 2024); POPE (EMNLP 2023, arXiv 2305.10355)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.17911
    what: OPERA evaluates CHAIR on 500 COCO-2014 val images with 'Please describe this image in detail.' and reports the same LLaVA-1.5 greedy decoder at CHAIR_S/CHAIR_I 20.6/6.2 with max_tokens=64 versus 45.0/14.7 with max_tokens=512, stating that 'the length of sequences can greatly affect the values of CHAIR' (https://arxiv.org/html/2311.17911). POPE was introduced because caption-based evaluation 'might be affected by the input instructions and generation styles of LVLMs' and instead polls Yes/No with random/popular/adversarial negatives (https://github.com/RUCAIBox/POPE).
    why: max_new_tokens is the second uncontrolled knob after resolution: lmms-eval amber_g uses 2048, OPERA-style papers 512, VLMEvalKit's Qwen wrapper 2048 and InternVL 4096. Every CHAIR/AMBER-g number in the report must carry its token cap and mean caption length, and the fixed protocol should pin 512 for captions and 128 for Y/N.
    code/data: https://github.com/shikiw/OPERA ; https://github.com/RUCAIBox/POPE

[11] Pascal (GTX 1060, sm_61) feasibility: bitsandbytes hardware table, PyTorch cu126 policy, and the FP16 throughput trap (2025-2026; bitsandbytes docs; PyTorch release notes v2.8.0 / v2.11.0; PyTorch cu126 wheel index; NVIDIA legacy CC list; NVIDIA Pascal Tuning Guide) verified=True
    url: https://huggingface.co/docs/bitsandbytes/main/en/installation
    what: bitsandbytes: NF4/FP4 and 8-bit quantization require CC 6.0+ (Pascal OK) but LLM.int8() requires 7.5+; PyPI wheels built with CUDA 11.8-12.6 target sm60 on Linux (sm50+sm60 on Windows) while the CUDA 12.8 and 13.x builds drop sm60 entirely. PyTorch 2.8.0: 'support for sm50 - sm60 architectures with CUDA 12.8 and 12.9 has been dropped' (https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/releases/tag/v2.8.0); 2.11.0: pip default is CUDA 13.0, which 'only supports Turing (SM 7.5) and newer', and 'Users with these older GPUs should use the CUDA 12.6 builds instead' (https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/releases/tag/v2.11.0); the cu126 index still lists torch-2.14.1+cu126 for cp310-cp315 on manylinux and win_amd64 (https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu126/torch/). NVIDIA lists GTX 1060 at CC 6.1 (https://developer.nvidia.com/cuda/gpus/legacy). The Pascal Tuning Guide: 'On GP104, FP16 throughput is lower, 1/64th that of FP32', while __dp4a/__dp2a INT8 dot products run at FP32 rate (https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/pascal-tuning-guide/index.html).
    why: A 4-bit HF path on the 1060 works only with cu126 torch plus a CUDA<=12.6 bitsandbytes build; load_in_8bit is impossible (needs 7.5+); flash_attention_2 (hardcoded by VLMEvalKit) and vLLM are unavailable; bf16 (hardcoded by both InternVL wrappers) is not native; and fp16 compute is a ~64x slowdown trap, so set bnb_4bit_compute_dtype=torch.float32 for a 3B model or use INT8 dp4a kernels through llama.cpp. Verify after install with torch.cuda.get_arch_list() containing sm_61.
    code/data: pip install torch --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu126 ; pip install bitsandbytes (CUDA 12.6 build)

[12] GGUF route on Pascal: llama.cpp libmtmd + llama-server OpenAI API, image-token flags, and the harness wrappers that can talk to it (2025-2026; llama.cpp docs/source; ggml-org HF GGUF repos; lmms-eval models/simple/openai.py; VLMEvalKit vlmeval/api/gpt.py; transformers GGUF docs) verified=True
    url: https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/docs/multimodal.md
    what: llama.cpp serves Qwen2-VL (2B/7B), Qwen2.5-VL (3B/7B/32B/72B) and InternVL2.5/3 (1B-14B) pre-quantized GGUFs (ggml-org/*-GGUF) through llama-server's OpenAI-compatible /v1/chat/completions with base64 image_url parts; server flags include --mmproj, --image-min-tokens / --image-max-tokens ('only used by vision models with dynamic resolution'), -ngl and -c (https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/blob/master/tools/server/README.md). ggml's CUDA backend defines GGML_CUDA_CC_DP4A 610, so a GTX 1060 gets INT8 dp4a kernels and no tensor-core MMA (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp/master/ggml/src/ggml-cuda/common.cuh). Sizes: Qwen2.5-VL-3B Q4_K_M 1.93 GB + mmproj Q8_0 845 MB (f16 1.34 GB); 7B Q4_K_M 4.68 GB + mmproj 853 MB (https://huggingface.co/ggml-org/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct-GGUF/tree/main, https://huggingface.co/ggml-org/Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct-GGUF/tree/main). transformers' GGUF loader maps no vision architecture (no qwen2vl/llava/internvl) and dequantizes (https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/main/en/gguf). lmms-eval's model 'openai' reads OPENAI_API_BASE/OPENAI_API_KEY with timeout 10 s, max_retries 5, max_size_in_mb 20 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/EvolvingLMMs-Lab/lmms-eval/main/lmms_eval/models/simple/openai.py); VLMEvalKit's GPT-style wrapper honors OPENAI_API_BASE or any http api_base with img_detail='low', img_size=-1, max_tokens 2048, temperature 0 (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit/main/vlmeval/api/gpt.py).
    why: This is the only verified way to run a quantized Qwen2.5-VL or InternVL3 inside either harness on sm_61: neither harness loads GGUF or exposes a 4-bit flag for these models, so serve the GGUF locally and point the harness's OpenAI-compatible wrapper at it. The 3B fits 6 GB with room for context; 7B Q4_K_M plus mmproj leaves under 0.5 GB and will need CPU offload. --image-max-tokens lets the GGUF run reproduce the same token budget as the HF protocol, but per-image token counts must be checked in the server log because llama.cpp's resize path is not transformers' smart_resize.
    code/data: https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp (MIT, not re-checked) ; https://huggingface.co/ggml-org/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct-GGUF ; https://huggingface.co/ggml-org/InternVL3-2B-Instruct-GGUF (listed in docs, page not opened)

-- gaps_and_opportunities --
  * No paper found that reports POPE, CHAIR or AMBER as an explicit function of input resolution or max_pixels/max_num for dynamic-resolution models: Qwen2-VL's Table 7 omits hallucination benchmarks, InternVL2.5's report has no tile-count ablation on HallusionBench/POPE, and pruning curves exist only for fixed-resolution LLaVA-1.5/NeXT. A token-budget sweep (Qwen2.5-VL-3B at ~100/256/391/640/1280 tokens; InternVL3-2B at max_num 1/2/6/12) on POPE + AMBER + HallusionBench, real vs synthetic, is unclaimed and runs on a 1060.
  * None of AIGCs Confuse AI Too, AutoHallusion or ODE reports or controls visual-token counts; square 512/1024 px generations versus 640x480 COCO photos receive 256 vs 1,792-3,328 tokens under InternVL and 324 vs 391 (or ~1,296 vs ~1,302 in VLMEvalKit) under Qwen2.5-VL. A resolution-matched replication (render or resize each synthetic image to its paired COCO image's exact dimensions) can confirm, shrink or re-attribute the published synthetic-vs-real gap.
  * The harness divergence itself is undocumented: the same model and the same POPE image get 391 tokens in lmms-eval but ~1,302 in VLMEvalKit (Qwen2.5-VL) and 3,328 vs 1,792 (InternVL). Quantifying how much leaderboard disagreement on POPE/HallusionBench this explains is a cheap, citable methods finding.
  * Interaction with quantization (the GHOST-Q sub-topic): nobody shows whether 4-bit error compounds at low token budgets; a 2x2 design (fp16/NF4 or Q8_0/Q4_K_M x native/capped tokens) is feasible on one GPU with the 3B model.
  * Object-size stratification: COCO annotations give box areas, so hallucination rate versus pixels-per-object at each token budget can be plotted directly, connecting the ICLR 2025 subject-size finding to object hallucination.
  * Link to the advisor's text-only reading: lmms-eval's pope_yes_ratio and POPE's three negative-sampling settings let the student test whether low token budgets shift the model toward confident 'yes' guessing (the Kalai et al. abstention framing) rather than uniform error, and whether an abstain/'cannot tell' option changes the curve.

-- recommended_for_this_project --
  * Models: Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct as the primary (fits 6 GB as NF4 on cu126 torch or as Q4_K_M GGUF) plus InternVL3-2B or -1B as the tiling contrast; use Qwen2.5-VL-7B only through llama.cpp with partial CPU offload or on a cloud GPU after a timed pilot. Skip Qwen3-VL for leaderboard comparison (OpenCompass numbers are vLLM-sampled) unless you run it yourself.
  * Fix the pixel grid before you fix anything else: generate synthetic images at 640x480 (SD 1.5 handles it; for FLUX/SDXL render 1152x864 and LANCZOS-downsample), or resize every synthetic image to the exact size of its paired COCO image, so real and synthetic sets get identical token counts under every model. Log tokens per sample: Qwen -> inputs['image_grid_thw'].prod(-1)//4; InternVL -> num_patches*256; llama-server -> the image-token count in its log.
  * Report two conditions. (A) Harness-default reproduction for a sanity check against published tables: VLMEvalKit Qwen2.5-VL entry (min_pixels=1280*28*28, max_pixels=16384*28*28, use_custom_prompt=False, max_new_tokens=2048, top_k=1, temperature=0.01) and InternVL max_num=6, compared to the OpenCompass leaderboard; or lmms-eval defaults (min_pixels=200704, max_pixels=1605632; InternVL max_num=12) compared to lmms-eval-based papers. (B) Controlled-budget sweep: greedy (do_sample=False, num_beams=1), max_new_tokens=128 for POPE/HallusionBench/AMBER-discriminative and 16 for MME, 512 for CHAIR/AMBER-generative, prompts exactly as lmms-eval ('\nAnswer the question using a single word or phrase.' for POPE/MME; '\nPlease answer yes or no.' for AMBER Y/N), fixed seed, batch_size 1, and token budgets set with min_pixels=max_pixels=N*28*28 (Qwen) or max_num in {1,2,6,12} (InternVL).
  * Exact HF-path command (lmms-eval): python -m lmms_eval --model qwen2_5_vl --model_args pretrained=Qwen/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct,min_pixels=200704,max_pixels=1605632,attn_implementation=sdpa --tasks pope,hallusion_bench_image,mme,amber_g --batch_size 1 --log_samples --output_path ./out ; add 4-bit by patching the wrapper's from_pretrained with BitsAndBytesConfig(load_in_4bit=True, bnb_4bit_quant_type='nf4', bnb_4bit_compute_dtype=torch.float32) because no CLI flag exists; install torch from https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu126 and confirm 'sm_61' in torch.cuda.get_arch_list() and a CUDA 12.6 bitsandbytes build.
  * Exact GGUF-path command: llama-server -hf ggml-org/Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct-GGUF -ngl 99 -c 4096 --temp 0 --image-min-tokens 256 --image-max-tokens 1280 --port 8080 ; then OPENAI_API_BASE=http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1 OPENAI_API_KEY=local python -m lmms_eval --model openai --model_args model_version=qwen2.5-vl-3b,timeout=600 --tasks pope --batch_size 1 ; for VLMEvalKit set OPENAI_API_BASE=http://127.0.0.1:8080/v1/chat/completions and add a GPT4V-style config entry with model='qwen2.5-vl-3b'. Pass the same --image-max-tokens that reproduces the HF budget and check the per-image token count in the server log.
  * VLMEvalKit patches needed on Pascal: attn_implementation='flash_attention_2' -> 'sdpa' and torch_dtype='auto' -> torch.float16/float32 in vlm/qwen2_vl/model.py; torch.bfloat16 -> float16 in vlm/internvl/internvl_chat.py; never use load_in_8bit (LLM.int8 needs CC 7.5+). Keep use_custom_prompt=False for Qwen2.5-VL so the prompt matches the leaderboard run.
  * Cost estimate procedure (no verified $/hour figures were obtainable this turn): time 50 POPE items per budget on the 1060 with the GGUF server, then scale to the full counts (POPE 3 x 3,000 Y/N over 500 images; AMBER 15,200 annotations over 1,004 images per Gao et al.; HallusionBench ~1.1k and MME ~2.4k questions, the last two unverified here). Rent a cloud GPU only if a full sweep exceeds roughly one to two days of wall-clock on the 1060, and price it from the provider's page at that time.
  * Cite as canonical: POPE (EMNLP 2023), CHAIR (Rohrbach et al. 2018, https://arxiv.org/abs/1809.02156, not opened this session), AMBER (https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.07397, not opened), HallusionBench (https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.14566, not opened), OPERA, SID, Qwen2-VL and InternVL2.5 reports, SparseVLM/VisionZip, Feather the Throttle, AIGCs Confuse AI Too, AutoHallusion, ODE.
  * Avoid: comparing against vendor model-card numbers (unknown pipeline and resolution), mixing lmms-eval and VLMEvalKit numbers in one table, fp16 compute dtype on Pascal, proprietary API baselines sent with detail='low' (a 512-px budget that is yet another hidden resolution), and any CHAIR/AMBER-g number reported without its max_new_tokens and mean caption length.

-- cautions --
  * The shared WebSearch budget was exhausted before this sweep started, so every item comes from direct fetches of URLs already known; newer 2025-2026 papers on resolution-vs-hallucination may exist and were not discoverable. Causal-HalBench could not be located or verified and is omitted; GHOST-Q was not checked.
  * All per-image token counts for 640x480 / 512x512 / 1024x1024 (391/324/1,369 Qwen2.5-VL; 300/256/1,024 Qwen3-VL; 1,302/1,296 VLMEvalKit-upscaled; 3,328/1,792/256/2,560/1,280 InternVL) are my arithmetic from the quoted smart_resize and dynamic_preprocess code, not numbers printed in any source; reproduce them by printing image_grid_thw / num_patches before relying on them.
  * VisionZip's LLaVA-NeXT POPE row was read twice: the first fetch returned 64.2 and the second (Table 12) 86.4/86.3/82.1/74.8; check the PDF before citing. PyramidDrop's CVPR 2025 and AutoHallusion's venue were not confirmed on the pages opened; SparseVLM's ICML 2025 venue is inferred from the arXiv keyword line.
  * PyTorch: torch-2.14.1+cu126 wheels verifiably exist, and the 2.11 notes say CUDA 12.6 builds are the path for Maxwell/Pascal, but no page opened states the compiled arch list of the 2.14.x cu126 wheel; confirm with torch.cuda.get_arch_list(). The releases-page fetch also mis-stated the 2.14.1 release date, so treat that tool summary as unreliable for dates.
  * The 1/64 FP16 figure is quoted for GP104 in the Pascal Tuning Guide; the GTX 1060 is GP106 (same CC 6.1 class) but the GP106 line itself was not quoted. bf16 behavior on sm_61 in current PyTorch was not verified; assume it is unsupported or slow and force float16/float32.
  * lmms-eval's 'openai' wrapper calls an encode-with-size-limit routine (resize_factor 0.75, min_side 100, 20 MB cap); confirm it does not shrink normal-size images, otherwise it silently changes the token budget. VLMEvalKit's API wrapper sends detail='low' by default, which llama.cpp ignores but OpenAI-class APIs honor.
  * lmms-eval has no CLI flag for 4-bit loading of Qwen2.5-VL and the --gen_kwargs syntax was not verified this session; the datasets now live under lmms-lab-encoder/* and the POPE variant/split included should be checked against the original 3 x 3,000-question design.
  * Memory headroom on 6 GB is an estimate, not a measurement: 3B NF4/Q4_K_M fits comfortably; 7B Q4_K_M + mmproj (about 5.5 GB) will need CPU offload; a 7B NF4 HF load will likely OOM.
  * Several license fields were not re-checked this session (VLMEvalKit, lmms-eval, llama.cpp, Qwen2.5-VL-3B weights); confirm before redistribution claims in the report.

-- confidence_notes -- High confidence on the harness defaults, prompt strings, generation settings and the token-count formulas (all quoted from source files or shipped configs). High confidence on the bitsandbytes/PyTorch/NVIDIA hardware statements (quoted from official docs, release notes and the cu126 wheel index). Medium confidence on the exact pruning-table numbers, which were extracted by the fetch tool from HTML tables (SparseVLM, VisionZip, PyramidDrop, OPERA, Qwen2-VL Table 7) and should be spot-checked against the PDFs. The derived token counts are deterministic given the quoted code but were not executed here. The absence claims (no resolution control in the three synthetic benchmarks; no POPE/CHAIR in FastV; no vision architectures in transformers' GGUF loader; no Qwen3-VL in llama.cpp's doc list) rest on reading the first ~100k characters of each page via the fetch tool, so a buried mention could have been missed.


######## ANGLE: FILL: Cross-lingual (Arabic) object hallucination: an unclaimed, regionally relevant angle ########
angle: Cross-lingual (Arabic) object hallucination in small open VLMs: what already exists (two multilingual POPE papers, an Arabic POPE/MME subset inside CAMEL-Bench, a QCRI counterfactual benchmark, an ArabicNLP 2026 shared task), what is still unclaimed (2025-26 small models, AMBER/HallusionBench-style Arabic sets, synthetic-vs-real crossed with language, Gulf/Najdi dialect, detector/mitigation transfer, abstention), what translation and inference would cost, and which regional venues fit the Week-13 timeline.

[1] Mitigating Multilingual Hallucination in LVLMs (MHR), Qu et al. (2024; arXiv (Aug 2024; publication venue not verified in this sweep)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.00550
    what: First paper to extend POPE to 13 languages (8 high-resource plus Ukrainian, Bulgarian, Turkish, Arabic, Korean), translating all queries with GPT-3.5 and curating them, then fixing the gap with a two-stage DPO framework. On POPE-COCO popular, LLaVA-1.5 drops from 86.0% accuracy in English to 16.8% in Ukrainian; the fix adds 19.0 points on average. Evaluated only on LLaVA-1.5 and CogVLM.
    why: This is the paper that already measured Arabic object hallucination on the same images, so the student cannot claim 'first Arabic POPE'. It also fixes the baseline story: on 2024 models, non-English prompts inflate hallucination massively, which is exactly the advisor's 'language prior beats visual evidence' framing. The open question is whether 2025-26 small models closed the gap.
    code/data: https://github.com/ssmisya/MHR (Apache-2.0; preprocessing/translate scripts; evaluation via lmms-eval; the multilingual POPE files themselves are not clearly released)

[2] CLAIM: Cross-Lingual Attention Intervention for multilingual object hallucination, Ye et al. (2025; ACL 2025 (main)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11073
    what: Near training-free method that aligns cross-modal attention patterns of non-English queries with English ones; +13.56 points average on multilingual POPE (up to +30 in Spanish) for LLaVA-1.5-7B, Qwen-VL-Chat and LLaVA-NeXT-7B. Questions were translated with Google Translate, refined, and checked by back-translation until label consistency was 100%. The paper evaluates Chinese, Spanish, Russian, Portuguese, Bulgarian, Hindi and German; Arabic is NOT in the paper, but the released dataset includes an Arabic split.
    why: Gives a ready-made Arabic POPE (27,000 rows per language, COCO+A-OKVQA+GQA x random/popular/adversarial) and a validation precedent (back-translation + label consistency). Because the paper never reports Arabic numbers, publishing Arabic results on modern models is still open. Its attention-misalignment finding is also a cheap mechanism to test on Arabic.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/HIT4Yzk/Multilingual_POPE (10 languages incl. Arabic; license and translation method not stated on the card); https://huggingface.co/datasets/HIT4Yzk/Multilingual_MME (9 languages, NO Arabic). No code link found on the arXiv page.

[3] CAMEL-Bench: A Comprehensive Arabic LMM Benchmark (incl. Arabic POPE and MME subsets) (2024-2025; NAACL 2025) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.18976
    what: 29K Arabic questions across 8 domains and 38 sub-domains; the Multimodal Understanding domain contains an 'Object Hallucination Evaluation' sub-domain of 997 questions drawn from POPE, CountBench and MMT-Bench-MI. English sources were translated with GPT-4o; translated items were screened by a Qwen-7B semantic-similarity check with manual review of failures, and native data by 20% random native-speaker sampling. The authors' HF collection exposes the pieces separately, e.g. ahmedheakl/arabic_pope (467 rows, COCO val2014, adversarial/popular/random) and ahmedheakl/arabic_mme (896 rows, 14 MME categories).
    why: Shows that Arabic object-existence yes/no questions already exist and have been scored for GPT-4o-class and Arabic-tuned models, which again removes 'first' claims but hands the student a reusable MIT-licensed Arabic POPE/MME seed and a documented translation-verification protocol to copy. The 467-row POPE subset is too small and undocumented (empty README, no license on the card) to be the main set.
    code/data: https://github.com/mbzuai-oryx/Camel-Bench (MIT); https://huggingface.co/collections/ahmedheakl/camel-bench-670750f3998395452cd3b7b1; https://huggingface.co/datasets/ahmedheakl/arabic_pope; https://huggingface.co/datasets/ahmedheakl/arabic_mme

[4] Once Correct, Still Wrong: Counterfactual Hallucination in Multilingual VLMs (M2CQA), Mousi et al. (QCRI) (2026; arXiv Feb 2026; listed as ACL 2026 on the dataset card (not verified elsewhere)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05437
    what: Introduces the CounterFactual Hallucination Rate (CFHR): the rate at which a VLM accepts a culturally plausible but visually unsupported statement, conditioned on having answered the true statement correctly. The M2CQA benchmark has 36,488 records over 9,122 images from 18 MENA countries, each image with one true and two counterfactual statements, in English, MSA, Levantine and Egyptian Arabic with stratified human verification. The abstract reports that CFHR 'rises sharply in Arabic, especially in dialects, even when true-statement accuracy remains high'.
    why: This is the closest existing Arabic VLM-hallucination study and it already demonstrates a language effect on the same images, but for cultural counterfactuals rather than object existence, and it covers Egyptian/Levantine, not Gulf/Najdi. The student should cite it as the motivating regional evidence and position object-existence (POPE/AMBER-style) plus Gulf dialect as the complementary, unclaimed cell. Its true/false-triple design is also a good template for synthetic-image statements.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/QCRI/M2CQA (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0); paper body could not be fetched (HTML 404, PDF over fetch limit), so tested model names and exact CFHR numbers are unverified

[5] ImageEval 2026 shared task at ArabicNLP 2026 (AynVQA Task 1b: image-grounded hallucination detection in English and MSA) (2026; ArabicNLP 2026 shared task (conference Oct 24-29, 2026)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30475
    what: Shared task with two tracks: AynVQA (spoken VQA plus hallucination detection) and CRAI-Bench (cultural accuracy of text-to-image). Task 1b gives an image and three statements, exactly one grounded, and systems must label each true/false in English and MSA; 3,000 train / 500 dev / 500 devtest / 1,000 test per language, drawn from M2CQA, scored by Contrastive Instability. 14 teams participated; data and scripts are released.
    why: Proves there is an active regional venue and community for Arabic multimodal hallucination, and provides 5,000 labeled EN/MSA items per language that the student can reuse as a real-image hallucination set alongside POPE. The 2026 test phase closed on Aug 9, 2026, so it is a template and dataset source, not a submission target this term.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/QCRI/ImageEval-ArabicNLP26 (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0, not gated); https://github.com/ImageEval2026/ImageEval2026-tasks; https://imageeval2026.github.io/

[6] SemEval-2027 Task 6: MMCultureQA, multilingual and multimodal culturally grounded spoken and visual QA (QCRI) (2026-2027; SemEval-2027 (workshop summer 2027)) verified=True
    url: https://mmcultureqa-semeval27.github.io/
    what: Textual-VQA and spoken-VQA tracks in MSA, Egyptian Arabic, Levantine Arabic and English (19 more languages announced), 10,000 train and 1,000 dev items per track, ranked by BERTScore F1. Training data was released Sept 23, 2026; evaluation runs Jan 10-31, 2027; system papers due Feb 2027; registration is still open as of Oct 2026. No explicit hallucination subtask.
    why: This is the most realistic 'tangible' follow-on for an undergraduate: the course report (due around Week 13, late Nov 2026) can become a hallucination-aware system plus a SemEval system-description paper submitted in Feb 2027. The Arabic/English paired questions on the same images also give a free second testbed for the language-prior analysis.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/QCRI/MMCQA-SemEval27 (CC-BY-NC-4.0); https://github.com/mmcultureqa-semeval27/mmcultureqa; task list at https://semeval.github.io/SemEval2027/tasks

[7] Overview of SHROOM-Visions 2026 (hallucination span detection in LVLM outputs) (2026; UncertaiNLP workshop at EMNLP 2026 (not SemEval)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25662
    what: Fourth SHROOM iteration: span-level detection and five-class classification of hallucinations in VLM outputs; 27 teams, 600+ submissions; best systems reach 0.58 character-level correlation and 0.51 IoU. Languages are Chinese, English, French and Italian only.
    why: Confirms the gap the task statement assumed (no Arabic in the main multimodal hallucination shared task) and corrects its venue (UncertaiNLP/EMNLP, not SemEval). An Arabic span-annotated set in this format would be a bigger-than-6-weeks but high-value follow-up; for now it is the citation that justifies the Arabic angle.
    code/data: Task data (SHEEP) referenced in the paper; no direct URL captured in this sweep

[8] Arabic-capable general VLM benchmarks already in VLMEvalKit: ALM-Bench, CVQA, MMMB/Multilingual MMBench, MTVQA (2024-2025; CVPR 2025 Highlight (ALM-Bench); NeurIPS 2024 D&B (CVQA); ICML 2025 (Parrot/MMMB); ACL 2025 Findings (MTVQA)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.16508
    what: ALM-Bench: 22,763 human-annotated QA pairs in 100 languages incl. Arabic, with MCQ, true/false, short and long VQA (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0). CVQA: 10k culturally grounded MCQs in 31 languages, including an Egyptian-Arabic/Egypt subset (mixed licenses). MMMB and MMBench_dev have Arabic splits registered in VLMEvalKit (MMMB_ar, MMBench_dev_ar); MTVQA covers AR among 9 languages (CC BY-NC 4.0). VLMEvalKit's yes/no registry (image_yorn.py) has only POPE, MME, HallusionBench, AMBER and VSR with no language variants.
    why: These give the student a sanity check that each candidate model actually understands Arabic prompts (run MMMB_ar vs MMMB_en first) and a way to separate 'does not know Arabic' from 'hallucinates more in Arabic'. The registry check also verifies that no Arabic POPE/AMBER/HallusionBench exists in VLMEvalKit, so an Arabic AMBER or HallusionBench would be a genuine resource contribution.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/MBZUAI/ALM-Bench; https://github.com/mbzuai-oryx/ALM-Bench; https://huggingface.co/datasets/afaji/cvqa; https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.02539 (Parrot, https://github.com/AIDC-AI/Parrot); https://github.com/bytedance/MTVQA; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit/main/vlmeval/dataset/image_yorn.py; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-compass/VLMEvalKit/main/vlmeval/dataset/image_mcq.py

[9] AIN: The Arabic INclusive Large Multimodal Model (MBZUAI) (2025; ACL 2025 (per arXiv comment)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.00094
    what: English-Arabic bilingual LMM built on Qwen2-VL and trained on 3.6M samples (35% authentic Arabic); the 7B model beats GPT-4o by 3.4 points averaged over CAMEL-Bench's eight domains. Weights released March 2025 under MIT; only a 7B variant is listed.
    why: The natural 'Arabic-tuned reference' model: if Arabic-specific instruction tuning narrows the Arabic-English hallucination gap relative to generic multilingual models, that is a clean finding. At 7B it will not run in fp16 on a 6 GB GTX 1060; use 4-bit quantization or a short cloud rental.
    code/data: https://github.com/mbzuai-oryx/AIN (MIT); weights at https://huggingface.co/MBZUAI/AIN

[10] Peacock (Arabic MLLM family + Henna benchmark) and Dallah (dialect-aware Arabic MLLM), UBC-NLP (2024; ACL 2024 (Peacock); arXiv 2024 (Dallah; venue not verified)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.01031
    what: Peacock: the first family of Arabic MLLMs with the Henna benchmark of GPT-4V-generated QA on images from 11 Arab countries. Dallah: a LLaMA-2-based Arabic MLLM handling six Arabic dialects, with MSA and dialectal evaluation. Neither paper evaluates hallucination.
    why: These are the canonical citations for Arabic MLLMs and dialect-aware prompting, and Dallah's six-dialect framing supports a Gulf/Najdi-vs-MSA prompt condition. They are too old and weak to be primary test models but belong in the related-work section.
    code/data: https://github.com/UBC-NLP/peacock; https://huggingface.co/UBC-NLP/Peacock; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UBC-NLP/Henna-Bench-Eval (license not shown in README); Dallah: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.18129 (no code link found)

[11] Qwen3-VL-2B/4B-Instruct and Qwen3.5-2B/4B model cards (Arabic support, license, hallucination scores) (2025-2026; Hugging Face model cards; GitHub) verified=True
    url: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct
    what: Qwen3-VL-4B (2025-10-15) and 2B (2025-10-21) are Apache-2.0, state OCR for 32 languages but list no text languages; their Qwen3 LLM backbone is documented as supporting 119 languages including eight Arabic varieties (Standard, Najdi, Levantine, Egyptian, Moroccan, Mesopotamian, Ta'izzi-Adeni, Tunisian). Qwen3.5-2B and 4B (Feb 2026, Apache-2.0, image-text-to-text, 262K context) state '201 languages and dialects' and report HallusionBench 58.0/51.3 (2B, thinking/non-thinking) and 65.0 (4B); no POPE numbers.
    why: These are the cheapest modern models that credibly read Arabic and fit a 6 GB card (2B in fp16 is borderline; 4B needs 4-bit GGUF). Qwen3.5's explicit Najdi/dialect coverage via Qwen3 makes a Gulf-dialect prompt condition defensible. Arabic support for Qwen3-VL is inferred from the Qwen3 blog, not the VL card.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-4B-Instruct; https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL (Apache-2.0); https://qwenlm.github.io/blog/qwen3/; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-2B; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B

[12] Gemma 4 E2B/E4B-it, InternVL3.5-2B and LFM2.5-VL-3B model cards (2025-2026; Hugging Face model cards) verified=True
    url: https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-E2B-it
    what: Gemma 4 E2B-it (HF repo created 2026-03-02; tech report arXiv 2607.02770): text+image+audio, 2.3B effective / 5.1B total parameters, 128K context, 'over 140 languages' with Arabic named, license shown as Apache-2.0 on the card and API; official qat-q4_0 GGUF repos exist for E2B and E4B. InternVL3.5-2B (Aug 2025, Apache-2.0, Qwen3-based) reports HallusionBench and a multilingual chart but its Arabic coverage is only in an image. LFM2.5-VL-3B (repo created 2026-08-11, license 'lfm1.0'/other) explicitly lists Arabic among 16 languages and reports POPE 88.7.
    why: Completes a 5-6 model panel spanning three families with explicit Arabic claims (Gemma 4, LFM2.5-VL, Qwen3.5) and one without (InternVL3.5), which lets the student test whether stated Arabic support predicts a smaller hallucination gap. LFM2.5-VL's published POPE 88.7 gives an English anchor to reproduce before translating.
    code/data: https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-E4B-it (listed, card not opened); https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-2B (Apache-2.0); https://huggingface.co/LiquidAI/LFM2.5-VL-3B (lfm1.0 license; check it permits research redistribution)

-- gaps_and_opportunities --
  * No published Arabic object-hallucination numbers exist for 2025-26 small open VLMs (Qwen3-VL, Qwen3.5, Gemma 4, InternVL3.5, LFM2.5-VL): MHR (2024) used LLaVA-1.5/CogVLM, CLAIM (ACL 2025) never reported Arabic, CAMEL-Bench scored GPT-4o-era and Arabic-tuned 7B models on a 997-item subset. 'Has the Arabic-English hallucination gap closed in modern 2-4B models?' is open and cheap.
  * Only POPE and MME have Arabic versions; no Arabic AMBER (existence/attribute/relation plus generative CHAIR), no Arabic HallusionBench, and VLMEvalKit's yes/no registry has no language variants. An Arabic AMBER-discriminative set (>14,000 short queries, Apache-2.0 source) would be a new, reusable resource for well under $1 of MT.
  * Nobody has crossed prompt language with synthetic vs real images. Synthetic scenes with controlled absent objects remove COCO co-occurrence priors, so comparing the Arabic-English yes-ratio gap on real vs synthetic images isolates the language-prior effect the advisor's framing predicts; this is the project's unique cell.
  * Gulf/Najdi Arabic prompts are untested anywhere: QCRI's M2CQA and MMCultureQA cover MSA, Egyptian and Levantine, and for cultural counterfactuals or VQA rather than object existence. NLLB-200 has ars_Arab (Najdi) and Qwen3 lists Najdi explicitly, and a KFUPM student has native-speaker access.
  * Transfer of English-trained detectors and mitigations to Arabic is unstudied: LLM-Check-style hidden-state/attention probes and training-free decoding (VCD/OPERA-style, or CLAIM's attention intervention) have only been validated on English or on five non-Arabic languages.
  * Abstention under language shift: Kalai et al. argue evaluations reward confident guessing; adding an explicit 'cannot tell' option to POPE/AMBER prompts and measuring abstention and yes-ratio by language (and by real vs synthetic image) has not been done for VLMs.
  * Venue timing: SemEval-2027 Task 6 (MMCultureQA) evaluation window Jan 10-31, 2027 and system papers Feb 2027 fits right after the Week-13 report; ArabicNLP shared tasks (ImageEval 2026 precedent) and SHROOM-Visions (no Arabic) show appetite for an Arabic multimodal hallucination contribution.
  * POPE's structure makes Arabic translation nearly free: the lmms-lab/POPE viewer shows only 86 unique question strings (one template over 80 COCO categories), so a complete, human-verified Arabic POPE is an afternoon's work; translation effort should go to AMBER's more varied attribute/relation queries.

-- recommended_for_this_project --
  * Core experiment (do this): same images, same models, English vs MSA vs Najdi prompts on POPE (9,000 COCO questions) and AMBER discriminative (>14,000 queries), on real images plus the project's synthetic set; report accuracy, F1, yes-ratio and paired per-image flips (McNemar), with the language gap as the headline. Models: Qwen3-VL-2B, Qwen3.5-2B/4B, Gemma 4 E2B/E4B-it, InternVL3.5-2B, LFM2.5-VL-3B, plus AIN-7B (4-bit or cloud) as the Arabic-tuned reference.
  * Arabic POPE: start from HIT4Yzk/Multilingual_POPE's Arabic split for speed, but regenerate your own by translating the 86 unique question strings and having two native speakers verify all of them (under 1 hour, $0); keep English and Arabic files row-aligned by question_id. Use ahmedheakl/arabic_pope only as a cross-check (467 rows, no license, empty README).
  * Arabic AMBER: translate the >14,000 discriminative queries with two independent systems, a local one (facebook/nllb-200-distilled-600M, CC-BY-NC, target arb_Arab and ars_Arab for Najdi, https://huggingface.co/facebook/nllb-200-distilled-600M; runs on the GTX 1060) and one batch LLM pass. Cost: about 23,000 queries x ~72 input tokens with instructions (1.7M) and ~25 Arabic output tokens (0.6M) is roughly $0.16 with gpt-5-nano Batch ($0.025 in / $0.20 out per MTok, https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/pricing) or roughly $0.23 with Claude Haiku 5.5 Batch ($0.05 in / $0.25 out per MTok after the 50% batch discount, https://claude.com/pricing); a back-translation pass doubles it, so the whole MT budget stays under $1. Avoid Helsinki-NLP/opus-mt-en-ar as the primary system (Apache-2.0 but BLEU 14.0 on Tatoeba).
  * Validation protocol (cheap, publishable): (1) back-translate every Arabic query with the other system and require the object/attribute term to map to the same label, CLAIM's 100%-consistency criterion; (2) stratified native-speaker review of a 300-query AMBER sample (100 each existence/attribute/relation) by two raters with reported agreement, mirroring CAMEL-Bench's 20% native sampling; (3) fix systematic issues once at the template level (definiteness, gender agreement, a single 'هل يوجد/توجد ... في الصورة؟' template); (4) add the Najdi variant only after MSA passes.
  * Compute plan: about 23,000 queries x 2-3 languages x 6-7 models is roughly 300-450k model calls. On the GTX 1060 (Pascal: no bf16, no FlashAttention, fp16 2B barely fits, 4B+ needs Q4 GGUF via llama.cpp) an estimated 1-3 s per call means 4-15 GPU-days unattended, so either subsample (POPE full + AMBER existence only) or rent: a RunPod RTX 4090 is $0.74-0.89/h (https://www.runpod.io/pricing) and should finish a 2-4B model in roughly 1-2 h with batched vLLM, so budget $20-50 total including retries. Reproduce LFM2.5-VL-3B's published English POPE 88.7 first as a pipeline check.
  * Bridge to the advisor's text-only list: train an LLM-Check-style probe (hidden states / attention kernel maps) on English POPE and test it zero-shot on Arabic prompts; add a 'cannot tell' abstention option (Kalai et al.) and report abstention rate by language; discuss MHR/CLAIM's cross-lingual attention misalignment as the VLM analogue of the 'language prior' argument.
  * Cite as prior work: Qu et al. 2024 (MHR), Ye et al. 2025 (CLAIM), CAMEL-Bench (NAACL 2025), Mousi et al. 2026 (M2CQA/CFHR), ImageEval 2026, SHROOM-Visions 2026 overview, POPE (https://github.com/RUCAIBox/POPE, MIT), AMBER (https://github.com/junyangwang0410/AMBER, Apache-2.0), ALM-Bench, AIN, Peacock, Dallah. Do not claim 'first Arabic POPE' or 'first Arabic VLM hallucination study'; claim 'first Arabic object-hallucination study on 2025-26 small open models, with synthetic images and a Gulf-dialect condition'.
  * Venue path: finish the Week-13 report as the guaranteed deliverable, then register for SemEval-2027 Task 6 (textual VQA track, MSA/Egyptian/Levantine/EN, eval Jan 10-31, 2027, CC-BY-NC-4.0 data) and submit a hallucination-aware system paper in Feb 2027; alternatively target ArabicNLP 2027 or an ACL/EMNLP 2027 workshop. Use the ImageEval-ArabicNLP26 Task 1b data (5,000 EN/MSA labeled items, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0) as a second real-image testbed.
  * Avoid: relying on InternVL3.5's Arabic support (not stated in text), DeepL or Google Cloud Translation cost claims without checking the live page, redistributing CC BY-NC-SA data under a more permissive license, and training-based mitigation (DPO/LoRA) on the 1060 within 6-8 weeks; keep mitigation to training-free decoding or prompting.

-- cautions --
  * The shared WebSearch budget was already exhausted when this sweep started, so every finding came from direct fetches of arXiv abstract/HTML pages, arXiv's own listing API, Hugging Face cards and API listings, GitHub READMEs and pricing pages. Papers whose abstracts do not use the words 'multilingual'/'Arabic' together with 'hallucination' may have been missed; a follow-up message can continue the search.
  * Qu et al. 2024 (MHR): publication venue not verified (cited here as arXiv 2408.00550). The 13-language list, GPT-3.5 translation and LLaVA-1.5 numbers come from the arXiv HTML.
  * Once Correct, Still Wrong (arXiv 2602.05437): HTML v1/v2 returned 404 and the PDF exceeded the fetch limit, so tested model names and exact CFHR numbers are unverified; 'ACL 2026' comes only from the QCRI/M2CQA dataset card.
  * HIT4Yzk/Multilingual_POPE: Arabic split exists (27,000 rows) but the card states no license and no translation method; CLAIM's Google-Translate + back-translation protocol was described for other languages. Treat the Arabic text as unvalidated until checked.
  * ahmedheakl/arabic_pope and arabic_mme: no license on the cards, empty README; they are pieces of the MIT-licensed CAMEL-Bench collection but the per-dataset terms were not confirmed.
  * Google Cloud Translation pricing page rendered as empty (JavaScript) and DeepL API pricing was not on the fetched page; the commonly quoted $20 per million characters and 500K free characters per month are NOT verified here. OpenAI (gpt-5-nano $0.05/$0.40 per MTok, Batch 50% off) and Anthropic (Haiku 5.5 $0.10/$0.50 per MTok, Batch 50% off) prices were read from the official pages on 2026-10-12 and may change.
  * Gemma 4 license appears as Apache-2.0 on both the HF card and the HF API (unlike Gemma 3's custom terms); verify the license_link in the repo before relying on it. The Gemma 4 E4B-it card itself was not opened.
  * Qwen3-VL Arabic support is inferred from the Qwen3 LLM blog (119 languages incl. eight Arabic varieties), not stated on the Qwen3-VL cards (which only state 32 OCR languages). InternVL3.5-2B's multilingual table is an image and could not be read.
  * Throughput figures (1-3 s per call on a GTX 1060; 1-2 h per model on an RTX 4090) are estimates, not measurements; llama.cpp GGUF vision support for Qwen3-VL and Qwen3.5 on Pascal GPUs was not verified in this sweep.
  * POPE '86 unique questions' and '9,000 rows' come from the lmms-lab/POPE dataset viewer statistics; AMBER counts ('1,004 generative, over 14,000 discriminative') come from its README. ImageEval 2027 or a new ArabicNLP hallucination task is not announced; only ImageEval 2026 (closed) and SemEval-2027 Task 6 (open) are confirmed.
  * Dallah's venue and code were not found on the arXiv page; Peacock/Henna license not shown in the README; ALM-Bench and M2CQA/ImageEval/MMCultureQA data are non-commercial (CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 or CC-BY-NC-4.0), fine for a course report and paper but not for redistribution under other terms.

-- confidence_notes -- High confidence on the existence, languages, sizes, licenses and dates of the items marked verified (all primary pages opened: arXiv abstracts/HTML, HF cards and API JSON, GitHub READMEs, VLMEvalKit source files, OpenAI/Anthropic/RunPod pricing). Medium confidence on the novelty verdict: within what could be fetched, no paper reports Arabic object-existence hallucination on 2025-26 small open models, none crosses language with synthetic images, and no Arabic AMBER/HallusionBench exists; but the search tool was unavailable, so this rests on arXiv-API abstract queries and HF listings rather than a full literature search. Low confidence on specifics flagged above: MHR's venue, the M2CQA paper's models and numbers, Multilingual_POPE's Arabic quality, Google/DeepL prices, and all GPU throughput estimates. Bottom line: an Arabic-vs-English object-hallucination comparison is not novel by itself (MHR 2024, CAMEL-Bench 2025), but it becomes novel and publishable when framed as modern small models + synthetic-vs-real images + Gulf/Najdi dialect + detector/abstention transfer, and the MT cost to get there is under $1 with a $20-50 inference budget if a cloud GPU is rented.


######## ANGLE: FILL: Statistical validity: error bars, paired tests and item-efficient subsets for a small benchmark run on a weak GPU ########
angle: Statistical validity and item-efficient evaluation for a small-GPU object-hallucination benchmark: error bars, paired real-vs-synthetic tests, human-verification sample sizes, clustering by image, and subset selection so a GTX 1060 can cover more models.

[1] Adding Error Bars to Evals: A Statistical Approach to Language Model Evaluations (Evan Miller, Anthropic) (2024; arXiv 2411.00640 (Anthropic research post)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00640
    what: Treats an eval as a sample from a question population and gives the formulas a reviewer expects: SE = sqrt(p(1-p)/n) for binary scores; clustered SEs for grouped questions (observed to be over 3x the naive SE on some evals); paired-difference SEs that exploit the covariance between two models' per-question scores (frontier-model correlations of 0.3-0.7 give a 'free' variance reduction); and a power formula n = (z_a/2 + z_b)^2 * Var / delta^2, with a worked example of 969 independent questions to detect a 3-point difference at 80 percent power and 5 percent alpha, hence the recommendation that new evals contain at least 1,000 questions. Also shows resampling answers beyond K~6 per question adds little.
    why: This is the template for every results table in the student's report: each POPE/AMBER/synthetic number gets an SE and CI, the real-vs-synthetic comparison is a paired difference, and questions sharing an image (POPE has 6 per image) are the clusters.
    code/data: Formulas only; companion post https://www.anthropic.com/research/statistical-approach-to-model-evals. Implement with numpy/statsmodels (no official repo).

[2] Position: Don't Use the CLT in LLM Evals With Fewer Than a Few Hundred Datapoints (Bowyer, Aitchison, Ivanova) + bayes_evals library (2025; ICML 2025 (spotlight position paper)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.01747
    what: Shows CLT intervals under-cover at small N (nominal 95 percent intervals cover only 92.5 percent at N=100, worse below) and that bootstrap intervals are also poorly calibrated at small N. Recommends Wilson score intervals (preferred over Clopper-Pearson) or a Beta(1,1) Beta-Bernoulli posterior for a single accuracy; a Bayesian paired comparison that models the correlation between two models' per-question outcomes; and, for clustered questions, a hierarchical Beta-Binomial model, the only method in their study with correct coverage across sample sizes.
    why: The student's per-category cells (e.g. 150 items per hallucination mode, per-object-class slices, the 217 real HaloQuest eval images) sit exactly in the few-hundred regime, so Wilson/Beta(1,1) intervals and the paired Bayesian comparison should be used there instead of plain CLT or bootstrap.
    code/data: https://github.com/sambowyer/bayes_evals (MIT): independent_intervals(), independent_comparisons(), paired_comparisons() returning pandas DataFrames with P(model i better than j).

[3] tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples (Maia Polo, Weber, Choshen, Sun, Xu, Yurochkin) (2024; ICML 2024) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14992
    what: Fits item response theory (IRT) on item-level responses from many models and clusters IRT embeddings into anchor points, so that 100 curated items estimate full-benchmark scores (MMLU 14K items -> 100) within about 2 points; builds on Anchor Points (Vivek et al., EACL 2024, https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.08638), where 1-30 anchor items rank models because correct-class confidence is correlated across models. The README includes a tutorial for making a tiny version of your own benchmark.
    why: Canonical citation for item-efficient evaluation, but the method needs a response matrix from dozens of models, which a single GTX 1060 cannot produce; the student should cite it and justify stratified sampling plus split-half reliability instead, or treat a 'tinyPOPE' as a cloud-credit follow-up.
    code/data: https://github.com/felipemaiapolo/tinyBenchmarks (MIT, pip install git+...); Hugging Face org https://huggingface.co/tinyBenchmarks (tinyMMLU, tinyGSM8k, tinyAlpacaEval, etc.; no VLM or hallucination sets).

[4] metabench: A Sparse Benchmark of Reasoning and Knowledge in Large Language Models (Kipnis, Voudouris, Schulze Buschoff, Schulz) (2025; ICLR 2025) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.12844
    what: Applies IRT to 28,632 items from six Open LLM Leaderboard benchmarks and keeps under 3 percent of them, reconstructing per-benchmark scores with 1.24 percent RMSE and the total score with 0.58 percent RMSE using responses from more than 5,000 models. Repository provides R IRT code, pre-selected item lists, adaptive-testing simulations and Zenodo data.
    why: Supplies the reviewer-facing evidence that a few-percent subset can retain ranking information, and a reusable IRT pipeline if the student later obtains item-level POPE/AMBER outputs from many public models.
    code/data: https://github.com/adkipnis/metabench (LICENSE file present; license type not visible in fetched README).

[5] Fluid Language Model Benchmarking (Hofmann, Heineman, Magnusson, Lo, Dodge, Sap, Koh, Wang, Hajishirzi, Smith; Ai2) (2025; arXiv 2509.11106 (Ai2)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11106
    what: Combines a 2PL IRT latent-ability model with adaptive item selection (computerized adaptive testing): on MMLU it reaches comparable validity with fifty times fewer items. The repository ships IRT fitting code, a fluid_benchmarking() adaptive loop, pretrained item parameters for six benchmarks and evaluation results for 102 LMs.
    why: The most recent, code-complete and permissively licensed IRT pipeline; porting it to POPE/AMBER would itself be a publishable 'tinyPOPE' contribution, but only if item-level responses from roughly 20+ VLMs are gathered (cloud or public logs), which should be costed before committing.
    code/data: https://github.com/allenai/fluid-benchmarking (Apache-2.0; pip install -e '.[irt]').

[6] Signal and Noise: A Framework for Reducing Uncertainty in Language Model Evaluation (Heineman et al., Ai2) (2025; arXiv 2508.13144) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13144
    what: Defines a benchmark's signal (how well it separates models) and noise (sensitivity to random variation across training steps/seeds) and shows the signal-to-noise ratio predicts whether small-scale decisions hold; interventions include switching metrics, dropping noisy subtasks and averaging checkpoints. Based on 375 models, 30 benchmarks and about 900K evaluation results, with 388,924 evaluations released.
    why: Gives a principled rule for which POPE/AMBER sub-splits (random/popular/adversarial; existence/attribute/relation) to keep when the item budget is cut, and vocabulary reviewers recognize for the 'does my subset still separate models' question.
    code/data: https://github.com/allenai/signal-and-noise (Apache-2.0); HF dataset allenai/signal-and-noise.

[7] LIME: Less Is More for MLLM Evaluation (Zhu et al.) - the only reduced POPE found (2024; arXiv 2409.06851 (v3, Oct 2024)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.06851
    what: Semi-automated curation for MLLM benchmarks: ten open-source MLLMs act as judges to label items easy (>=6 correct), middle (3-5) or hard (<=2); easy items are removed, zero-pass items are checked by GPT-4V and humans, and text-answerable items are dropped. Overall 76 percent fewer samples and 77 percent less time; POPE is cut from 9,000 to 443 items, with the paper claiming 95 percent of POPE is 'noisy or erroneous' (too easy).
    why: This is difficulty filtering, not IRT, so absolute accuracies are not comparable to full POPE, but it is a ready-made hard POPE subset a GTX 1060 can run on many models, and a baseline against which the student's own stratified subset can be compared.
    code/data: https://github.com/kangreen0210/LIME ; dataset https://huggingface.co/datasets/LIME-DATA/POPE (443 rows, Apache-2.0).

[8] Measuring the Measurers: Quality Evaluation of Hallucination Benchmarks for LVLMs (HQM / HQHBench; Yan, Zhang, Yuan, Shan, Chen) (2024 (revised Feb 2026); arXiv 2406.17115 (cs.CV)) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.17115
    what: Reliability is test-retest (Pearson correlation of scores across 20 checkpoints of 9 LVLMs re-run with different random seeds) and parallel-forms (yes/no questions rewritten into negated forms with flipped answers, options shuffled, captions paraphrased); validity is content validity (fraction of items surviving manual review) and criterion validity (Pearson vs human evaluation). Table I: POPE test-retest 0.9996, parallel-forms 0.3563, content 0.84, criterion 0.9634; AMBER-d 0.9986 / 0.3636 / 0.90 / 0.9321; AMBER-g 0.9378 / 0.5333; MMHal 0.8829 / 0.8833; GAVIE 0.9091 / 0.9136; HQH 0.9977 / 0.9856 / 0.9556. HQH has 4,000 image-instruction pairs (500 x 8 types), fully manually reviewed, with no inter-annotator statistic, CIs or significance tests.
    why: This is the psychometric protocol to replicate on the student's own synthetic benchmark (negated parallel form, two-seed retest, manual content check), and the source of the POPE 0.36 number, which measures yes/no acquiescence bias under negation, not instability from drawing a 500-item subset.
    code/data: https://github.com/HQHBench/HQHBench (HQH.json, image_data.json, GPT-4o evaluate.py; no license file seen).

[9] Evaluating Object Hallucination in Large Vision-Language Models (POPE; Li, Du, Zhou, Wang, Zhao, Wen) (2023; EMNLP 2023) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.10355
    what: 500 MSCOCO val images with at least 3 ground-truth objects, 6 yes/no questions per image (3 present, 3 absent) under random, popular and adversarial negative sampling: 3,000 questions per setting, 9,000 in total; metrics are accuracy, precision, recall, F1 and Yes-ratio. Reports no CIs, SEs or significance tests, only a +/- std across prompt templates for LLaVA.
    why: The 6-questions-per-image design means items are clustered by image: with intra-image correlation rho the design effect is 1 + 5*rho (1.5 at rho = 0.1, 2.0 at rho = 0.2), so SEs must be clustered by image or bootstrapped at image level, and any subset should keep whole images (e.g. 250 images x 6 = 1,500 items), which also preserves the yes/no balance.
    code/data: lmms-eval tasks pope, pope_adv, pope_pop, pope_random, pope_full (verified folder listing); original repo RUCAIBox/POPE not re-fetched in this sweep.

[10] VHTest: Visual Hallucinations of Multi-modal Large Language Models (Huang, Liu, Guo, Gong) - plus AMBER's annotation protocol (2024; arXiv 2402.14683 (VHTest); AMBER arXiv 2311.07397) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.14683
    what: VHTest: 1,200 visual-hallucination instances (8 modes x 150) built on generated images with manually designed and manually verified questions and answers; agreement reported as Fleiss' kappa = 0.958 from 4 independent annotators on 100 randomly selected yes/no instances; no CIs or significance tests. AMBER (https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.07397): 1,004 images (COCO 2014 test + Unsplash), 15,216 discriminative queries (4,924 existence, 7,628 attribute, 1,664 relation) plus 1,004 generative; 'multiple groups of annotators' with manual inspection and correction, but no annotator count, kappa, CIs or tests.
    why: Sets the convention the student must meet or beat: a random audit of at least 100 items by 2-4 annotators with a chance-corrected agreement statistic; the recommended audit below (200 items, kappa target >= 0.8, Wilson CI on label accuracy) is stricter than both and cheap.
    code/data: Data described in the papers; GitHub repos for VHTest and AMBER not re-fetched in this sweep.

[11] HaloQuest: A Visual Hallucination Dataset for Advancing Multimodal Reasoning (Wang, Bingham, Yu, Le, Luong, Ghiasi; Google) (2024; ECCV 2024) verified=True
    url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.15680
    what: 7,748 VQA examples mixing real (Open Images) and Midjourney-generated images: train 7,140 (2,985 real / 4,155 synthetic), eval 608 (217 real / 391 synthetic). Reports that model scores on generated images correlate r = 0.97 with scores on real images and an Auto-Eval judge correlating r = 0.99 with human raters; no per-item paired tests or CIs in the abstract.
    why: Closest precedent for the student's 'synthetic vs real' claim, but its evidence is a model-level correlation, not a matched per-item test; the student can go further with one-to-one real/synthetic twins and McNemar or paired-bootstrap tests, and can reuse the 608-item eval split as an external check.
    code/data: https://github.com/google/haloquest (code Apache-2.0, data CC-BY-4.0; external image licenses vary).

[12] Statistical toolkit: statsmodels, SciPy, scikit-learn, krippendorff, R MESS::power_mcnemar_test, and lmms-eval --limit (2025-2026 (current documentation); Library documentation) verified=True
    url: https://www.statsmodels.org/stable/generated/statsmodels.stats.contingency_tables.mcnemar.html
    what: statsmodels: mcnemar(table, exact=True, correction=True) for paired yes/no items; proportion_confint(count, nobs, method='wilson') for CIs; samplesize_confint_proportion(proportion, half_length) for the human-audit sample; inter_rater.fleiss_kappa(table, method='fleiss') with aggregate_raters (no variance or tests implemented); NormalIndPower().solve_power for unpaired two-proportion power. SciPy: permutation_test(..., permutation_type='samples') for paired permutation tests and bootstrap(data, statistic, paired=True, method='BCa', n_resamples=9999) for paired CIs (pass image-level arrays to get a cluster bootstrap). scikit-learn cohen_kappa_score(y1, y2, weights=None|'linear'|'quadratic'); krippendorff (pip install krippendorff; nominal/ordinal/interval; GPL-3.0). R MESS::power_mcnemar_test (methods normal, exact, cond.exact; cites Duffy 1984 and Fagerland, Lydersen & Laake 2013) to cross-check sample sizes. lmms-eval (MIT core, Apache-2.0 tasks) provides pope/pope_adv/pope_pop/pope_random/pope_full and hallusion_bench_image tasks and a --limit N flag.
    why: Everything the methods section needs is off the shelf; the student only writes a short script for the image-clustered bootstrap and the McNemar sample-size formula.
    code/data: https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.stats.permutation_test.html ; https://docs.scipy.org/doc/scipy/reference/generated/scipy.stats.bootstrap.html ; https://www.statsmodels.org/stable/generated/statsmodels.stats.proportion.proportion_confint.html ; https://www.statsmodels.org/stable/generated/statsmodels.stats.proportion.samplesize_confint_proportion.html ; https://www.statsmodels.org/stable/generated/statsmodels.stats.inter_rater.fleiss_kappa.html ; https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.metrics.cohen_kappa_score.html ; https://github.com/pln-fing-udelar/fast-krippendorff ; https://rdrr.io/cran/MESS/man/power_mcnemar_test.html ; https://github.com/EvolvingLMMs-Lab/lmms-eval

-- gaps_and_opportunities --
  * None of the object-hallucination benchmark papers verified here (POPE, AMBER, HallusionBench abstract, VHTest, HQM/HQH, HaloQuest abstract, ODE abstract) reports confidence intervals, standard errors or significance tests; all give point estimates. Adding Wilson CIs, image-clustered SEs and paired tests is a near-zero-compute differentiator that a 2026 reviewer will expect.
  * No IRT / anchor-point 'tiny' version of POPE, AMBER or HallusionBench was verified. LIME's 443-item POPE is difficulty filtering with 10 judge models, not IRT, and it drops easy items so scores are not comparable to full POPE. A tinyPOPE/tinyAMBER built with the Fluid Benchmarking or tinyBenchmarks code would be publishable on its own but needs item-level responses from roughly 20-30 VLMs (cloud credits or public per-item logs); cost this before committing.
  * Subset (split-half) reliability of POPE and AMBER has never been measured: HQM's reliability numbers come from seed re-runs and negated parallel forms, not from item subsets. The student can measure it for free from their own runs by repeatedly splitting the item set in halves and reporting Spearman rank correlation of model orderings and the fraction of pairwise flips as a function of subset size, which directly answers 'how small can the benchmark be'.
  * Image-clustered standard errors (Miller) and hierarchical Bayesian intervals for clustered questions (Bowyer et al.) have not been applied to VLM hallucination evals even though POPE has 6 questions per image and AMBER has about 15 discriminative queries per image.
  * A matched per-item real-vs-synthetic design with paired tests does not exist: HaloQuest reports only a model-level r = 0.97 between generated and real images. Twin items (same object, same question polarity, real COCO image vs synthetic counterpart) tested with McNemar and paired bootstrap would be new and are cheap because pairing cuts the needed item count by roughly 2-3x.
  * The advisor's text-only readings (Kalai et al. on evaluations rewarding guessing, HalluLens' NonExistentRefusal) suggest reporting an abstention-aware metric with CIs; no verified VLM object-hallucination benchmark does this, so a yes/no/unsure protocol with CIs on the abstention rate bridges the reading list to the multimodal setting.

-- recommended_for_this_project --
  * Design the synthetic benchmark as matched pairs: every synthetic item has a real-image twin with the same object, question wording and polarity. Then the headline question 'is hallucination higher on synthetic images?' is a paired McNemar test on per-item correctness (statsmodels mcnemar exact=True), and the yes-ratio question is the same test on the raw yes/no answers. Pairing needs ~2-3x fewer items than independent sets: by the computed table, a 5-point difference needs ~470 pairs at 15 percent discordance but ~900-1,100 items per group if unpaired.
  * Sample sizes (alpha = 0.05 two-sided, 80 percent power, normal-approximation McNemar formula n = [z_a*sqrt(psi) + z_b*sqrt(psi - delta^2)]^2 / delta^2, delta = accuracy difference, psi = discordance rate; cross-checked with a 4,000-replicate Monte Carlo of the continuity-corrected test): 5-point difference -> 312 / 469 / 626 / 940 pairs at psi = 0.10 / 0.15 / 0.20 / 0.30; 3-point difference -> 870 / 1,306 / 1,742 / 2,614 pairs. Unpaired two-proportion (85 vs 80 percent): 906 per group; (85 vs 82): 2,402 per group. Run a 200-pair pilot to estimate psi, then fix n. A practical target is 1,500 pairs (250 images x 6 questions): >= 80 percent power for 5 points under any plausible psi <= 0.3 and ~85 percent for 3 points at psi = 0.15.
  * Compute budget argument: 1,500 items is 6x fewer forward passes than full POPE (9,000), which is what lets the GTX 1060 cover 5-6 open 2-8B VLMs and 2-3 mitigation methods instead of 2 models; reviewers will accept 1,500 paired items with CIs and tests more readily than 9,000 items on two models without error bars. Use lmms-eval --limit or a custom item list to run the stratified subset; leave per-question timings to the compute agent.
  * Error bars on every number: report n and a Wilson 95 percent CI (statsmodels proportion_confint method='wilson') for each accuracy, F1-adjacent proportion and yes-ratio; for any cell under a few hundred items (per hallucination type, per object class, the 217 real HaloQuest eval images) use Wilson or a Beta(1,1) posterior per Bowyer et al., never plain +/-1.96*sqrt(p(1-p)/n) and not bootstrap on tiny cells.
  * Cluster by image: POPE-style items share images, so compute SEs with Miller's clustered formula or a cluster bootstrap that resamples images (scipy bootstrap with paired=True over image-level arrays, method='BCa', 9,999 resamples), and report the design effect 1 + (m-1)*rho (expect 1.3-2.0 for m = 6). Draw subsets by whole image, stratified by setting (random/popular/adversarial) and polarity, so clustering stays explicit and yes/no balance is preserved.
  * Model-vs-model and method-vs-baseline comparisons on identical items: exact McNemar plus a paired-bootstrap CI on the difference plus bayes_evals paired_comparisons() probability; apply Holm correction when more than one comparison is reported; run each model twice (two seeds, or greedy decoding stated) and report test-retest agreement so reviewers see the noise floor.
  * Subset validity: do not attempt IRT/tinyBenchmarks on the 1060 (needs item responses from dozens of models). Instead show split-half reliability of your subset: 100 random disjoint halves, Spearman rank correlation of model orderings and fraction of pairwise flips; optionally run LIME's 443-item hard POPE as a secondary subset, noting it is difficulty-filtered. Cite tinyBenchmarks, metabench and Fluid Benchmarking as the reason item-efficient evaluation is legitimate.
  * Human verification: audit a uniform random sample of 200 items (statsmodels samplesize_confint_proportion: ~203 items give +/-3 points at 95 percent label accuracy; 73 give +/-5; 457 give +/-2) with 2 annotators (3-4 if available); report percent agreement and Cohen's kappa (sklearn) or Fleiss' kappa / Krippendorff's alpha for 3+ raters, target kappa >= 0.8 (VHTest reports 0.958 on 100 items with 4 annotators), adjudicate disagreements, and report the Wilson CI of post-adjudication label accuracy. For synthetic images add an explicit absent-object check (annotator confirms the queried object is truly absent) since generators insert unrequested objects.
  * Psychometrics a la HQM on the student's own set: build the negated parallel form (flip every answer), re-run with a second seed, and report Pearson test-retest and parallel-forms correlations across the evaluated models plus content validity (fraction surviving the audit). Beating POPE's 0.3563 parallel-forms reliability with a polarity-balanced design is a concrete, quotable result.
  * Cite: Miller 2024 (SE, clustering, pairing, power), Bowyer et al. 2025 (small-N intervals), tinyBenchmarks / Anchor Points / metabench / Fluid Benchmarking (item efficiency), Signal and Noise (subtask selection), HQM (reliability protocol), POPE/AMBER/VHTest/HaloQuest (annotation and synthetic-real conventions), Fagerland et al. 2013 via the MESS docs for McNemar variants. Avoid: describing HQM's 0.36 as subset instability; reporting a single accuracy without n; using LIME-POPE numbers as if comparable to full POPE.

-- cautions --
  * The shared WebSearch budget was already exhausted when this agent ran, so every item comes from direct WebFetch of primary URLs known in advance; no arXiv sweep for 2025-2026 papers was possible. 'No object-hallucination paper reports CIs' and 'no tinyPOPE exists' therefore mean 'none verified among the papers checked', not 'none exists'. A follow-up search is advisable before making these claims in the paper.
  * ReactBench ('two-stage review') and GHOST ('human absence check'), named in the brief, could not be located or verified in this sweep; no arXiv ids or numbers for them are included, and they should not be cited until someone opens the primary source.
  * Correction to the benchmarks agent's framing: HQM's POPE parallel-forms reliability of 0.3563 was computed from negated-question forms across 20 checkpoints of 9 LVLMs (test-retest 0.9996 used different random seeds). It is evidence of yes/no response bias, not that a 500-item subset reorders models. HallusionBench does not appear in HQM's Table I (verified rows: POPE, AMBER-d, AMBER-g, OpenCHAIR, MMHal, GAVIE, HQH).
  * Sample sizes were computed by this agent (script in the scratchpad, power_calc.py) using the standard normal-approximation McNemar formula as implemented by R MESS::power_mcnemar_test method='normal' (commonly attributed to Connor 1987; that attribution was not verified because PubMed was cookie-walled) and confirmed by Monte Carlo. They depend strongly on the discordance rate psi, which must be estimated from a pilot; with the 6-per-image clustering the effective n is smaller by the design effect.
  * Miller's 969 / 'at least 1,000 questions' figure assumes independent questions and a 3-point effect; Bowyer et al. show both CLT and bootstrap intervals under-cover below a few hundred items, so the paired-bootstrap recommendation here is for n >= ~500 pairs; use Wilson or Beta(1,1) intervals for smaller cells.
  * tinyBenchmarks' Hugging Face datasets show more rows than 100 (e.g. tinyMMLU 385) because they bundle extra splits; '100 examples' is the paper's claim. metabench's license was not visible in the fetched README; HQHBench's README has no license; krippendorff is GPL-3.0; LIME-DATA/POPE is Apache-2.0 but the underlying COCO images carry their own terms.
  * lmms-eval's pope*.yaml and hallusion_bench_image.yaml tasks were verified from directory listings; an AMBER task in lmms-eval was not verified (the task-list doc page returned 404). The RUCAIBox/POPE, VHTest and AMBER GitHub repositories were not re-fetched.
  * Fluid Benchmarking and Signal and Noise are text-LLM studies (MMLU-style); applying them to VLM yes/no hallucination items is an extrapolation the student should state explicitly.

-- confidence_notes -- High confidence on items whose primary pages were fetched and whose numbers are quoted directly: Miller 2024 (969 questions, >3x clustered SEs, 0.3-0.7 correlations), Bowyer 2025 (92.5 percent coverage at N=100, Wilson/Beta(1,1)/paired Bayesian/clustered recommendations, MIT library), tinyBenchmarks (100 items, MIT), metabench (<3 percent, 1.24 percent RMSE, ICLR 2025), Fluid (50x fewer items, Apache-2.0), Signal and Noise (375 models, 30 benchmarks, Apache-2.0), LIME (POPE 9,000 -> 443, 10 judge models, Apache-2.0 dataset), HQM (Table I values, negation-based parallel forms, 20 checkpoints), POPE (500 images x 6 questions, 9,000 total, no CIs), VHTest (Fleiss kappa 0.958, 4 annotators, 100 items, 1,200 instances), AMBER (1,004 images, 15,216 discriminative queries, no kappa), HaloQuest (7,748 examples, 608 eval = 217 real + 391 synthetic, r = 0.97). Medium confidence on the negative claims (no CIs in object-hallucination papers; no IRT subset of POPE/AMBER/HallusionBench) because only about seven papers could be checked without search. The sample-size table is the agent's own computation (formula plus Monte Carlo) and should be re-run with the pilot discordance rate.
